non-linear periodic waves on the Einstein cylinder
Athanasios Chatzikaleas, Jacques Smulevici

Abstract
. Motivated by the study of small amplitudes non-linear waves in the Anti-de-Sitter spacetime and in particular the conjectured existence of periodic in time solutions to the Einstein equations, we construct families of arbitrary small time-periodic solutions to the conformal cubic wave equation and the spherically-symmetric Yang–Mills equations on the Einstein cylinder ℝ × 𝕊 3 \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3} . For the conformal cubic wave equation, we consider both spherically-symmetric solutions and complexed-valued aspherical solutions with an ansatz relying on the Hopf fibration of the 3 3 -sphere. In all three cases, the equations reduce to 1 1 + 1 1 semi-linear wave equations. Our proof relies on a theorem of Bambusi–Paleari for which the main assumption is the existence of a seed solution, given by a non-degenerate zero of a non-linear operator associated with the resonant system. For the problems that we consider, such seed solutions are simply given by the mode solutions of the linearized equations. Provided that the Fourier coefficients of the systems can be computed, the non-degeneracy conditions then amount to solving infinite dimensional linear systems. Since the eigenfunctions for all three cases studied are given by Jacobi polynomials, we derive the different Fourier and resonant systems using linearization and connection formulas as well as integral transformation of Jacobi polynomials. In the Yang–Mills case, the original version of the theorem of Bambusi–Paleari is not applicable because the non-linearity of smallest degree is nonresonant. The resonant terms are then provided by the next order non-linear terms with an extra correction due to backreaction terms of the smallest degree non-linearity and we prove an analogous theorem in this setting. Finally, we emphasize that, in view of the KAM type method used, the time periodic solutions that we construct exist only when the small perturbative parameter belongs to a Cantor–like set.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Stability/instability of the Anti-de-Sitter spacetime

The Anti-de-Sitter spacetime (AdS) is the maximally symmetric solution to the vacuum Einstein equations with a negative cosmological constant

\mathrm{Ric}(g)=-\Lambda g,\quad\Lambda<0.

(1.1)

Given
\Lambda<0
, this is the simplest or trivial solution to (
1.1
), in a similar sense that the Minkowski or de-Sitter spacetimes are the trivial solutions to the vacuum Einstein equation with
\Lambda=0
or
\Lambda>0
. While the stability properties of the Minkowski or de-Sitter spacetimes are now well-understood
[
18
,
24
]
, the study of pertubations of AdS is still an active subject of research. One important aspect is that, AdS or, more generally, spacetimes which are asymptotically AdS are not globally hyperbolic. Hence, any evolution problem for these solutions is only well-posed after boundary conditions are imposed at the conformal boundary. Two naturally opposite classes of boundary conditions are the fully reflective and dissipative boundary conditions.
In the reflective case, we expect, as originally conjectured by Dafermos-Holzegel in
[
21
]
and independently by Anderson in
[
1
]
, that AdS is unstable. Strong evidence for this instability was first presented by Bizoń-Rostworowski
[
10
]
, which pioneered the study of the spherically-symmetric Einstein-Klein-Gordon system using numerical and Fourier based analysis and proposed weak turbulence as the non-linear source of instability. Recently, a proof of instability for the spherically Einstein-Vlasov
1
1

1

Moschidis has further announced similar results for the spherically-symmetric Einstein-scalar-field system
[
30
]
.
system has been obtained in the work of Moschidis
[
28
,
29
]
, based on a physical space mechanism.
In the dissipative case, one has strong decay of solutions for the linearized Einstein equations
[
25
]
and this should lead to stability even at the non-linear level.

1.2.
The time-periodic solutions of Rostworowski–Maliborski

In the reflective case, parallel to the study of the instability conjecture, an interesting class of solutions was introduced by Rostworowski–Maliborski in
[
27
]
, where they constructed pertubatively and numerically small data, time-periodic solutions of the spherically-symmetric Einstein-scalar field system. They furthermore suggested, based on their numerical analysis, that these solutions should enjoy stronger stability properties than the original AdS space. The present paper is directly motivated by this work. We indeed prove here the existence of arbitrary small time-periodic solutions for various toy models, which mimic certain properties of non-linear waves in the AdS space.

1.3.
The conformal wave and the Yang–Mills equations

More precisely, we study the conformal wave and the Yang-Mills equations on the Einstein cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}
. The AdS spacetime is conformal to half of the Einstein cylinder, so that solutions to the conformal wave and the Yang-Mill equations on the AdS spacetime can be mapped to solutions on the whole Einstein cylinder with a certain reflection symmetry at the equator.
The conformal cubic wave equation on the Einstein cylinder can be written as

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,\omega)+\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}\phi(t,\omega)-\phi(t,\omega)=\left|\phi(t,\omega)\right|^{2}\phi(t,\omega),

(1.2)

for a scalar field
\phi:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
with
\phi=\phi(t,\omega)
. We will consider perturbations around the static solution
\phi_{0}=0
and for simplicity with zero initial velocity.

In the spherically symmetric case, the initial value problem for (
1.2
) reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\left(\partial_{t}^{2}+L\right)u=f(u),\hskip 5.0pt(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\pi),\\
u_{0}(x)=u(0,x),\\
u_{1}(x)=\partial_{t}u(0,x)=0,\end{cases}

(1.3)

for a scalar field
u:\mathbb{R}\times(0,\pi)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
u=u(t,x)
and

\displaystyle Lu:=-\Delta^{ss}_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}u+u,\hskip 5.0ptf(u):=-u^{3},

(1.4)

where

\displaystyle-\Delta^{ss}_{\mathbb{S}^{3}}u:=-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(\sin^{2}(x)\partial_{x}u\right)

stands for the spherically symmetric Laplace–Beltrami operator on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
.

When the spherical symmetry assumption is removed
[
22
,
3
]
, we use an ansatz based on
Hopf coordinates
2
2

2

We would like to thank Oleg Evnin who suggested the Hopf coordinate ansatz.

(\eta,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\in\left[0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right]\times\left[0,2\pi\right)\times\left[0,2\pi\right)
rather than the standard spherical coordinates.
The Laplace–Beltrami operator on
\mathbb{S}^{3}
in these coordinates reads as

\displaystyle\Delta_{(\eta,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}^{\mathbb{S}^{3}}\chi=\partial_{\eta}^{2}\chi+\left(\frac{\cos\eta}{\sin\eta}-\frac{\sin\eta}{\cos\eta}\right)\partial_{\eta}\chi+\frac{1}{\sin^{2}\eta}\partial_{\xi_{1}}^{2}\chi+\frac{1}{\cos^{2}\eta}\partial_{\xi_{2}}^{2}\chi.

While in principle, the Fourier expansion with respect to
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
of a solution
\chi(t,\eta,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
to (
1.2
) may include all possible admissible frequencies, we will pick a fixed pair of frequencies
(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and force the Fourier expansion to excite only this particular pair by implementing the ansatz

\displaystyle\chi(t,\eta,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})=u(t,\eta)e^{i\mu_{1}\xi_{1}}e^{i\mu_{2}\xi_{2}}.

(1.5)

This leads us to consider the following initial value problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\left(\partial_{t}^{2}+\mathsf{L}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\right)u=\mathsf{f}(u),\hskip 5.0pt(t,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\left(0,\pi/2\right),\\
u_{0}(\eta)=u(0,\eta),\\
u_{1}(\eta)=\partial_{t}u(0,\eta)=0,\\
\end{cases}

(1.6)

for a scalar field
u:\mathbb{R}\times\left(0,\pi/2\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
u=u(t,\eta)
and

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}u:=-\partial_{\eta}^{2}u-\left(\frac{\cos\eta}{\sin\eta}-\frac{\sin\eta}{\cos\eta}\right)\partial_{\eta}u+\left(\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}}{\sin^{2}\eta}+\frac{\mu_{2}^{2}}{\cos^{2}\eta}+1\right)u,\hskip 5.0pt\mathsf{f}(u)=-u^{3}.

(1.7)

Finally, we consider the spherically symmetric equivariant Yang–Mills equation on the Einstein cylinder for the
SU(2)
connection, which reduces
[
12
]
to the study of

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}^{2}\phi(t,x)+\partial_{xx}^{2}\phi(t,x)+\frac{\phi(t,x)}{\sin^{2}(x)}=\frac{\phi^{3}(t,x)}{\sin^{2}(x)},

(1.8)

for a scalar field
\phi:\mathbb{R}\times(0,\pi)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
with
\phi=\phi(t,x)
. We will study perturbations of the static solution
\phi_{0}=1
to the above equation. Writing

\phi(t,x)=1+\sin^{2}(x)u(t,x),

we are led to the following initial value problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\left(\partial_{t}^{2}+\mathfrak{L}\right)u=\mathfrak{f}(x,u),\hskip 5.0pt(t,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\pi),\\
u_{0}(x)=u(0,x),\hskip 5.0pt\omega\in(0,\pi)\\
u_{1}(x)=\partial_{t}u(0,x)=0,\hskip 5.0pt\omega\in(0,\pi)\end{cases}

(1.9)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}u:=-\frac{1}{\sin^{4}x}\partial_{x}\left(\sin^{4}x\partial_{x}u\right)+4u,\hskip 5.0pt\mathfrak{f}(x,u):=-3u^{2}-\sin^{2}(x)u^{3}.

(1.10)

1.4.
Main results and general strategy

In the following, we use the abbreviations:

•

CW: conformal cubic wave equation in spherical symmetry, that is (
1.3
)–(
1.4
),

•

CH: conformal cubic wave equation out of spherical symmetry in Hopf coordinates according to the ansatz (
1.5
), that is (
1.6
)–(
1.7
),

•

YM: Yang–Mills equation in spherical symmetry, that is (
1.9
)–(
1.10
),

and study the evolution of the perturbations

\displaystyle u:\mathbb{R}\times I\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},\hskip 5.0ptu=u(t,x),\hskip 5.0ptI=\begin{cases}(0,\pi),&\text{ for CW}\\
\left(0,\pi/2\right),&\text{ for CH}\\
(0,\pi),&\text{ for YM}\end{cases}

driven by the partial differential equations

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{t}^{2}+\mathbf{L}\right)u=\mathbf{f}(x,u),\hskip 5.0pt(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}\times I,

(1.11)

subject to the initial data with zero initial velocity,

\displaystyle\begin{cases}u_{0}(x)=u(0,x),\hskip 5.0ptx\in I,\\
u_{1}(x)=\partial_{t}u(0,x)=0,\hskip 5.0ptx\in I.\end{cases}

Here, the linear operators and the non–linearities are given respectively by

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}u

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-\frac{1}{\sin^{2}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(\sin^{2}(x)\partial_{x}u\right)+u,&\text{ for CW}\\
-\partial_{x}^{2}u-\left(\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}-\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}\right)\partial_{x}u+\left(\frac{\mu_{1}^{2}}{\sin^{2}x}+\frac{\mu_{2}^{2}}{\cos^{2}x}+1\right)u,&\text{ for CH}\\
-\frac{1}{\sin^{4}x}\partial_{x}\left(\sin^{4}x\partial_{x}u\right)+4u,&\text{ for YM}\end{cases}

(1.12)

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}(x,u)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}-u^{3},&\text{ for CW}\\
-u^{3},&\text{ for CH}\\
-3u^{2}-\sin^{2}(x)u^{3},&\text{ for YM}\end{cases}

(1.13)

Associated to the linear operators given by (
1.12
), one can introduce natural Hilbert spaces and with suitable definitions for their domains (Section
3
), the linear operators are then all self–adjoint operators with compact resolvent.
In order to simplify the presentation below, we denote by
\{e_{n}(x):n\geq 0\}
the set of eigenfunctions of any of these operators
3
3

3

Of course, the eigenfunctions are different for the different operators, so that this is just a generic name.
and by
\{\omega_{n}:n\geq 0\}
the set of corresponding eigenvalues. Recall the facts that, in all three models considered, the sequences
\{\omega_{n}:n\geq 0\}
are all strictly increasing with
\omega_{n}\sim n
as
n\rightarrow+\infty
.

We also denote by
\Phi^{t}(\xi)
the flow associated to any of the linearized equations with initial data
\left(u_{t=0},\partial_{t}u_{t=0}\right)=\left(\xi,0\right)
. If
\xi_{n}
denote the Fourier coefficients of
\xi
associated to the eigenbasis
\{e_{n}(x):n\geq 0\}
, then

\displaystyle\Phi^{t}(\xi)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\cos(t\omega_{n})\xi_{n}e_{n}(x).

(1.14)

To state our result, we need to introduce a set of frequencies verifying a certain Diophantine condition
[
2
]
. Given
0<\alpha<1/3
, define

\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}:=\left\{\omega\in\mathbb{R}:\hskip 5.0pt|\omega\cdot l-\omega_{j}|\geq\frac{\alpha}{l},\hskip 5.0pt\forall(l,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2},\hskip 5.0ptl\geq 1,\hskip 5.0pt\omega_{j}\neq l\,\right\}.

(1.15)

Consider any of the problems
CW
,
CH
or
YM
and let
e_{\gamma}
be one of the eigenfunction to the corresponding linear operator. On top of
\alpha
, the statements of our results depend on the constant
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the index of the eigenfunction, and
s>0
, which defines the Sobolev space
4
4

4

The definition of the
H^{s}
spaces is adapted to each problem, see Section
2
.

H^{s}
where the solutions will belong to. Our assumptions are slightly different depending on the problems addressed.

Assumptions 1.1
.

Specifically, we make the following assumptions.

•

CW: We take
\gamma\in\{0,1,2,\dots,\}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 2
.

•

CH: We take
\gamma\in\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 2
. Moreover, we assume that the parameters
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
appearing in (
1.6
) verify
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=\mu
with
\mu
either sufficiently large, or
\mu\in\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}
.

•

YM: We take
\gamma\in\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}
and
s\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 3
.

Remark 1.2

(Range of
\gamma
)
.

We note that our proof is based on closed formulas for the Fourier coefficients, integrals that quantify the mode couplings. Although we derive these formulas uniformly with respect to
\gamma
(see Section
5
), we also need to check the validity of particular non–linear conditions depending on the Fourier coefficients. On the one hand, for the CW model, the Fourier coefficients have a relatively simple closed formula. Hence, there is no need to restrict the range of
\gamma
and we establish the validity of the conditions needed uniformly with respect to
\gamma
. On the other hand, for the CH and YM models, the complexity of the Fourier coefficients requires to restrict the range of
\gamma
to any finite set. Since the smaller the range is, the easier one can verify our computations, we fix
\gamma\in\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}
solely for the purpose of computing and verifying all computations in the manuscript by hand. However, we believe that our result stated below holds true also for larger values of
\gamma
. For the convenience of the reader, we attach Mathematica files that can help the reader to both easily verify our computations for small
\gamma
as well as derive and verify the analogous closed formulas for larger values of
\gamma
.

Under Assumptions
1.1
, we prove the following result.

Theorem 1.3

(Main result 1: Existence of time–periodic solutions to all three models bifurcating from various 1–modes)
.

Let
(\gamma,s)\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}
verify the Assumptions
1.1
and let
e_{\gamma}
be the eigenfunction to the corresponding linear operator. Also, let
0<\alpha<1/3
and
\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
be the corresponding set of frequencies, defined in (
1.15
). Then, there exists a family
\{u_{\epsilon}:\epsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}\}
of time-periodic solutions to either CW, CH or YM where
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}
is an uncountable set that has zero as an accumulation point. In addition, each element
u_{\epsilon}
has the following properties:

(1)

u_{\epsilon}
has period
T_{\epsilon}=2\pi/\omega_{\epsilon}
with
\omega_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
being
\epsilon-
close to one,

(2)

u_{\epsilon}\in H^{1}\left(\left[0,T_{\epsilon}\right];H^{s}\right)
.

(3)

u_{\epsilon}
stays for all times close to the solution to the linearized equation with initial data
\left(u_{t=0},\partial_{t}u_{t=0}\right)=\left(\epsilon\kappa_{\gamma}e_{\gamma},0\right)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\|u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot)-\Phi^{t\omega_{\epsilon}}\left(\epsilon\kappa_{\gamma}e_{\gamma}\right)\right\|_{H^{s}}\lesssim\epsilon^{2},

where
\kappa_{\gamma}
is a normalization constant.

For the CW and CH models, the results above are proven using a theorem of Bambusi and Paleari
[
2
]
, while for the YM model, the original version of their theorem (stated at Theorem
2.4
below) is not applicable and we will adapt their work. To explain this, we follow
[
2
]
and consider any of the models above in the Fourier space by projecting the equations on the eigenbasis
\{e_{n}:n\geq 0\}
, so that, schematically, the equations take the form

\ddot{u}^{j}(t)+(Au(t))^{j}=(f(u))^{j},\hskip 5.0ptj\geq 0,

(1.16)

where
u=\{u^{j}:j\geq 0\}
denotes the array of the Fourier coefficients,
A
is a multiplication operator defined by
(Au)^{j}=\omega_{j}^{2}u^{j}
and
(f(u))^{j}
are the coefficients of the non-linearity written in Fourier space, which takes the form of a polynomial in the
u^{j}
. In addition, we assume that

\displaystyle f(u)=f^{(0)}(u)+f^{(1)}(u),

where
f^{(0)}
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
r\geq 2
and
f^{(1)}
is a polynomial of degree at least
r+1
. Then, one looks for solutions
u(t)
to (
1.16
) where
u(t)
belongs to the Hilbert space

\displaystyle l_{s}^{2}:=\left\{u=\{u^{j}:j\geq 0\}:\hskip 5.0pt|u|^{2}_{s}<\infty\right\}

associated with the norm

\displaystyle|u|^{2}_{s}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}|u_{j}|^{2}.

Besides some regularity considerations, the main theorem in
[
2
]
asserts that given any non-degenerate zero of the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\xi=A\xi+\langle f^{(0)}\rangle(\xi),\quad\xi=\left\{\xi^{j}:j\geq 0\right\}\in l_{s}^{2},

where
\langle f^{(0)}\rangle(\xi)
denotes the average in time of the non-linearity
f^{(0)}
along the linearized flow, one can construct a family of small data periodic in time solutions, with properties similar to those stated in Theorem
1.3
.
The operator
\mathcal{M}
is in fact linked to the resonant system associated to the original equation. If
u(t)
is periodic in time with period
\omega
, let
q
be defined by
u(t)=q(\omega t)
and let
L_{\omega}
be the operator

L_{\omega}q=\omega^{2}\frac{d}{dt^{2}}q+Aq.

The proof of
[
2
]
is based on a Lyapunov-Schmidt decomposition
q=q_{\perp}+v
, with
v\in\ker L_{1}
and
q_{\perp}\in(\ker L_{1})^{\perp}
, together with the projections of the equations onto the range and kernel of
L_{1}
, leading to the so-called
P
-equation

\displaystyle L_{\ssomega}q_{\perp}

\displaystyle=Pf(v+q_{\perp}),

(1.17)

and
Q
-equation

\displaystyle(1-\omega^{2})Av

\displaystyle=Qf(v+q_{\perp}).

(1.18)

The Diophantine condition (
1.15
) is then used to solve the
P
-equation, while the non-degeneracy assumption and an implicit function argument is used to solve the
Q
-equation.

For the CW and CH models, one easily verifies that the eigenfunctions
\kappa_{n}e_{n}
, where
\kappa_{n}
is an appropriate rescaling, are all zeroes of
M
, so that the main difficulty is to establish the non-degeneracy condition, i.e., to prove that the kernel of
d\mathcal{M}(\kappa_{n}e_{n})
is trivial. In the YM case, however, the non-linearity contains both quadratic and cubic terms, so that a priori, only the quadratic terms would contribute to the definition of the operator
\mathcal{M}
. On the other hand, it turns out that, the average along the flow of the quadratic terms actually vanishes identically, leading to a degenerate, linear operator
\mathcal{M}
. Thus, we introduce a replacement for the operator
\mathcal{M}
, that takes into account also the cubic terms. However, the quadratic terms still play a role in this modified operator. Indeed, the solution to the
P-
equation roughly takes the form

\displaystyle q_{\perp}(v)=q_{\perp,\text{quadratic}}(v)+q_{\perp,\text{cubic}}(v)+\dots

where the term
q_{\perp,\text{quadratic}}(v)
arises from the quadratic non-linearity, and after substituting
q_{\perp}(v)
into the
Q-
equation, these terms will generate new additional cubic terms. Thus, in some sense, the backreaction of the quadratic terms into the
Q-
equation must also be taken into account. This type of difficulty, where the contribution of the lowest degree part of the non-linearity is non-resonant, has been treated in some situations, see for instance
[
4
]
, Section 4.2 and
[
6
]
, Section 1.2.3, that consider equations of the form
-\partial_{tt}u+\partial_{xx}u=u^{2p}+\mathcal{O}(u^{2p+1})
.
Here, we prove a modified abstract version of the Bambusi-Paleari’s theorem which we then apply to the YM model.

Theorem 1.4

(Main result 2: Modification of the Bambusi-Paleari theorem for the YM model)
.

Consider the partial differential equation in the Fourier space

\displaystyle\ddot{u}^{j}(t)+(\mathfrak{A}u(t))^{j}=\left(\mathfrak{f}\left(u(t)\right)\right)^{j},\hskip 5.0ptj\geq 0,

(1.19)

where the dots stand for derivatives with respect to time and
\mathfrak{A}
is a positive multiplication self–adjoint operator with pure point and resonant spectrum
\{\ssomega_{j}^{2}>0:j\geq 0\}
with
\ssomega_{j}\sim j
, as
j\rightarrow\infty
, defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}:\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})\simeq l_{s+2}^{2}\longrightarrow l_{s}^{2},\hskip 5.0pt(\mathfrak{A}u)^{j}:=\ssomega_{j}^{2}u_{j},

with
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
being its maximal domain of definition
5
5

5

Later, we will take
l_{s+1}^{2}
instead of
l_{s}^{2}
as our base Hilbert space, so that we will consider
\mathfrak{A}
as an operator from
l_{s+3}^{2}\longrightarrow l_{s+1}^{2}
. This allows for the construction of classical solutions, instead of solutions defined via the Duhamel formula or duality.
. In addition, assume that the non–linearity is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}(u)=\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)+\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(u),

where each
\mathfrak{f}^{(k)}
is a homogeneous polynomial of order
k
which is well-defined and smooth in
l_{s}^{2}
, with
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
being non–resonant, that is

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\rangle(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\pPhi^{t}\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(x)\right)\right)dt=0,

(1.20)

for all initial data
x
, where
\pPhi^{t}(x)
denotes the solution to the linearized equation with initial data
(x,0)
. Furthermore, define the modified operator

\displaystyle{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\pm\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)+\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi),

where
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)
is given by Lemma
2.13

and let
\xi_{0}\in l_{s+3}^{2}
be initial data such that

•

\xi_{0}
is a zero of
{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}
,

\displaystyle{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}\left(\xi_{0}\right)=0,

•

and
\xi_{0}
satisfies the following non–degeneracy condition

\displaystyle\ker\left(d{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}\left(\xi_{0}\right)\right)=\{0\}.

Also, for some
0<\alpha<1/3
, define
\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
according to (
1.15
). Then, there exists a family
\{u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot):\epsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}\}
of time–periodic solutions to (
1.19
) where
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}
is an uncountable set that has zero as an accumulation point. In addition, each element
u_{\epsilon}
has the following properties:

(1)

u_{\epsilon}
has period
T_{\epsilon}=2\pi/\omega_{\epsilon}
with
\omega_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
being
\epsilon-
close to one,

(2)

u_{\epsilon}\in H^{1}\left(\left[0,T_{\epsilon}\right];l_{s}^{2}\right)
.

(3)

u_{\epsilon}
stays for all times close to the solution to the linearized equation with initial data
\left(u_{t=0},\partial_{t}u_{t=0}\right)=\left(\epsilon\kappa_{\gamma}e_{\gamma},0\right)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot)-\Phi^{t\omega_{\epsilon}}\left(\epsilon\kappa_{\gamma}e_{\gamma}\right)\right|_{s}\lesssim\epsilon^{2},

1.5.
Remarks

We note the following remarks.

(Minimal periods) Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
give no information on the minimal periods
T_{\epsilon}
of the time–periodic solutions
u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot)
. However, one can relate
T_{\epsilon}
to the minimal period
T_{0}
of the solutions to the linearized system with 1–mode initial data, see Theorem 5.3 in
[
2
]
.

(Cantor–like set) We emphasize that the time periodic solutions we construct exist only when the small perturbative parameter belongs to a Cantor–like set (of
0
measure). This set together with the Diophantine condition introduced in Theorem
1.3
are closely related to the presence of small divisors in the perturbation series around equilibrium points, a classical topic in the context of KAM theory in infinite dimensions. Although this type of conditions is essential in proving the existence of time–periodic solutions as in
[
2
]
, it can be removed in some very special cases, see for example
[
14
]
). On the other hand, we note that the numerical constructions
[
27
,
15
,
23
]
do not seem to see the small divisors obstructions.

(Proof) The proof of Theorem
1.4
follows the general strategy of
[
2
]
, the main and essential difference being the backscattering contribution of the quadratic terms. An alternative approach to ours would be to find a normal form, in the spirit of
[
37
]
, that allows us to eliminate the quadratic terms and then apply the original result of
[
2
]
.

(The works of Berti–Bolle) In the works
[
5
,
6
,
4
]
, a different strategy, based on variational methods, was introduced to solve the
Q
-equation (
2.11
), instead of the implicit function theorem as in
[
2
]
. This in particular leads to a strong improvement in
[
6
]
concerning the size of the frequency set, using an extra Nash-Moser iteration. We have not implemented this here for simplicity and leave a possible implementation of this improvement for future works. The works
[
5
,
6
]
also treat the case of non-resonant quadratic terms, as we have here in the Yang-Mills case, in the specific case of the wave equation on an interval with Dirichlet boundary conditions.

(Regularity of the solutions) The solutions constructed here are
H^{1}
in time with values in
H^{s}
, with
s
arbitrarily large but fixed a priori. A posteriori, one can then use the equation to obtain additional regularity properties of the solutions. For instance, one easily has
\partial_{t}^{2}u\in L_{t}^{2}H_{x}^{s-1}
. Since some of the estimates depend a priori on the value of
s
, we cannot directly take
s=\infty
but it is likely that a refinement of the methods presented would lead to such an improvement.

(Jacobi polynomials) One of the difficulties to proving Theorem
1.3
comes from the fact that the eigenfunctions
e_{n}(x)
of the linearized operators are given by Jacobi polynomials, instead of simpler explicit functions. This fact is not specific to our model problem and is a general feature of non-linear wave equations on AdS-like background. In particular, in the CH and YM models
6
6

6

The eigenfunctions for the CW case are given by Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind. The derivation of the resonant system in this case had been previously addressed in
[
7
]
.
, the computation and the analysis of the Fourier coefficients associated to the resonant terms are non–trivial and constitute one of the contributions of this paper. To this end, we use linearization and connection formulas as well as particular Mellin transforms for the Jacobi polynomials. On the one hand, a linearization formula (also called addition formula) represents a product of two orthogonal polynomials with some parameters as a linear combination of orthogonal polynomials of the same kind with the same parameters. On the other hand, a connection formula represents a single orthogonal polynomial with some parameters as a linear combination of orthogonal polynomials of different kind with new parameters. In both cases, these are computationally efficient only in the case where the coefficients in the expansions are known in closed formulas. These computations also motivate our choice of
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}=\mu
for the CH model, since in this case, the eigenfunctions are reduced to Gegenbauer polynomials, a special class of Jacobi polynomials with additional algebraic properties that lead to closed formulas for the linearization and connection coefficients described above. Moreover, we also use particular Mellin transforms of Gegenbauer polynomials. These are integral transforms that may be regarded as the multiplicative version of the Laplace transform.

(Mathematica files) For the CH and YM models, Theorem
1.3
ensures the existence of time–periodic solutions bifurcating only from finitely many 1–mode initial data. As stated in Remark
1.2
, this is solely for the purpose of computing and verifying all computations in the manuscript by hand. Furthermore, one can use Mathematica
[
26
]
to verify that our result still holds true also for larger values of
\gamma
. For the convenience of the reader, we attach the Mathematica files needed that can help the reader to both easily verify our computations for small
\gamma
as well as derive and verify the analogous computations for larger values of
\gamma
.

1.6.
Previous works

The conformal wave equation (
1.2
) was introduced as a toy problem for the study of non-linear waves in confined geometries in
[
7
]
and has since been the study of several works, see
[
14
,
9
,
8
]
. In particular, in
[
14
]
, it was proven that solutions emanating from the first mode
e_{0}
, stay proportional to
e_{0}
for all times and are periodic in time. The fact these data do not excite further modes is however specific to the first mode and to this equation.

Concerning the well-posedness theories for the different models, since we do not focus here on low regularity solutions, we will simply recall that global well-posedness holds for the conformal cubic wave equation in the energy space, while the Yang-Mills equations in curved geometry have been shown to be globally well-posed in
H^{2}\times H^{1}
in
[
19
,
16
]
and on AdS with reflective boundary conditions in
[
17
]
. We were motivated for the study of the Yang-Mills model by
[
11
]
.

Since the pioneering work
[
27
]
, there has been many investigations of time-periodic solutions for non-linear equations with completely resonant spectrum
[
5
,
32
,
4
,
6
]
.
For the conformal wave equations, there exist also several constructions of time-periodic, weak solutions, via the variational techniques first introduced by Rabinowitz
[
34
,
33
]
, see
[
13
,
39
]
.

[

]
, during the BIRS workshop "Time-like Boundaries in General Relativistic Evolution Problems", held at Oaxaca, Mexico, in July 2019, as well as Riccardo Montalto (University of Milan) for useful discussions concerning KAM theory. 1.8.
Organization of the paper

We split the paper into the following sections:

•

Section
2
: we describe the methods we are about to use. For CW and CH, we will use the original version
[
2
]
of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem (Theorem
2.4
). However, for YM, as explained above, we need to revise the original version and establish an extension of their result (Theorem
2.5
) as stated in Theorem
1.4
. In particular, we define the operators
\mathcal{M}
and
{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}
that determine the “special” initial data leading to time–periodic solutions.

•

Section
3
: we study the linear eigenvalue problems where the linearized operators are given by (
1.12
). As it turns out, the associated eigenfunctions are given by Jacobi polynomials and this is a common feature with the Einstein-Klein-Gordon system in spherical symmetry
[
27
]
.

•

Section
4
: we express the partial differential equations (
1.11
) in the Fourier space and obtain infinite dimensional systems of coupled harmonic oscillators.

•

Section
5
: we define and study the mode couplings given by the Fourier coefficients. Specifically, we derive explicit closed formulas for all the Fourier coefficients on resonant indices.

•

Section
6
: we study 1–mode initial data. In particular, we show that these modes satisfy the resonant systems (are zeros of the operators
\mathcal{M}
and
{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}
defined in Section
2
). In addition, we derive their differentials at these modes.

•

Section
7
: firstly, we derive the crucial non–degeneracy conditions for the 1–mode initial data. As it turns out, these are non–linear conditions for the Fourier coefficients. Then, we use the analysis on the Fourier coefficients from Section
5
to rigorously establish these conditions and prove the existence of time–periodic solutions as stated in Theorem
1.3
.

2.
The method of Bambusi–Paleari revisited

In order to establish Theorem
1.3
and construct our time–periodic solutions we rely on the method Bambusi–Paleari
[
2
]
. This is an effective method to construct families of small amplitude time–periodic solutions close to a resonant equilibrium point for semi–linear partial differential equations.

2.1.
The original version of the theorem

Let
s\geq 0
be a real number and define the Hilbert space
l_{s}^{2}
to be the space of sequences such that

\displaystyle u=\{u^{j}:j\geq 0\},\hskip 5.0pt|u|_{s}^{2}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}|j^{s}u^{j}|^{2}<\infty.

We endow
l_{s}^{2}
with the natural inner product associated with the norm
|\cdot|_{s}

and consider the following differential equation in
l_{s}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\ddot{u}^{j}+(\mathfrak{A}u)^{j}=\left(\mathfrak{f}\left(u\right)\right)^{j},\hskip 5.0ptj\geq 0,

(2.1)

where the dots denote derivatives with respect to time. Here,
\mathfrak{A}
is a positive multiplication self–adjoint operator with pure point and resonant spectrum
\{\ssomega_{j}^{2}:j\geq 0\}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}:\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})\longrightarrow l_{s}^{2},\hskip 5.0pt(\mathfrak{A}u)^{j}:=\ssomega_{j}^{2}u_{j}

and
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
stands for its maximal domain of definition endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})}^{2}:=|u|_{s}^{2}+|\mathfrak{A}u|_{s}^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left|\xi^{j}\right|^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left|\ssomega_{j}^{2}\xi^{j}\right|^{2}.

Moreover, we assume that
\mathfrak{A}
and
f
verify the following conditions:

(1)

The injection of
\left(\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A}),\|\,.\,\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})}\right)
into
l_{s}^{2}
is compact.

(2)

The non-linearity
\mathfrak{f}(u)
can be decomposed into

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}(u)=\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}(u)+\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}(u).

(2.2)

(3)

The lowest degree term
\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}(u)
is a homogeneous polynomial of order
r\geq 2
and is bounded operator from
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
to
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
with the domain
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
being invariant under
\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}
.

(4)

The highest degree term
\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}(u)
(treated perturbatively as an error term) has a zero of order
r+1
at zero, is differentiable in
l^{2}_{s}
, its differential is Lipschitz and verify the estimate

\displaystyle\left|d\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}(u_{1})-d\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}(u_{2})\right|_{s}\leq C\epsilon^{r-1}\left|u_{1}-u_{2}\right|_{s},

for all
u_{1},u_{2}\in l_{s}^{2}
with
|u_{1}|_{s}\leq\epsilon
and
|u_{2}|_{s}\leq\epsilon
.

Remark 2.1
.

In our case, the conditions above are obtained by starting from any of the equations (
1.3
), (
1.6
), (
1.9
) and projecting them on the eigenfunctions to the corresponding linear operator. Specifically, condition (
1
) follows automatically from the fact
\ssomega_{j}\sim j
, as
j\rightarrow\infty
, while conditions (
3
) and (
4
) that refer to the non-linearities in the Fourier space essentially follow from the fact the original non-linearities are smooth and that the Sobolev spaces of sufficiently high regularity form an algebra, see Section
2.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{e}_{n}
be the eigenfunctions to the associated linearized operators. On the Fourier side, these can be identified with
\mathfrak{e}_{n}=\{\delta^{i}_{n}:i\geq 0\}\in l^{2}_{s}
. Then, for any initial data,

\displaystyle\xi=\{\xi^{n}:n\geq 0\}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi^{n}\mathfrak{e}_{n},\hskip 5.0pt

we denote by

\displaystyle\pPhi^{t}(\xi)=\left\{\xi^{n}\cos(\ssomega_{n}t):n\geq 0\right\}

its linear flow, that is the solution to the initial value problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ddot{u}^{n}(t)+\ssomega_{n}^{2}u^{n}(t)=0,\hskip 5.0ptt\in\mathbb{R}\\
u^{n}(0)=\xi^{n},\hskip 5.0pt\dot{u}^{n}(0)=0.\end{cases}

We note that
\pPhi^{t}(\xi)=\pPhi^{-t}(\xi)
. Moreover, we define the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left(\xi\right):=\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}\rangle(\xi),

where

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}\rangle(\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\pPhi^{-t}\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]dt

is the average of
\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}
along the linear flow. Notice that the highest degree term
\mathfrak{f}^{(1)}
does not contribute to the definition of the operator
\mathcal{M}
. In
[
2
]
, Bambusi–Paleari used a Lyapunov–Schmidt decomposition together with averaging theory and established the existence of a family of small amplitude time–periodic solutions with frequencies that satisfy the strong Diophantine condition

\displaystyle\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}:=\left\{\ssomega\in\mathbb{R}:\hskip 5.0pt|\ssomega\cdot l-\ssomega_{j}|\geq\frac{\alpha}{l},\hskip 5.0pt\forall(l,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2},\hskip 5.0ptl\geq 1,\hskip 5.0pt\ssomega_{j}\neq l\right\}.

(2.3)

Remark 2.2

(Accumulation to one)
.

For
0<\alpha<1/3
, the set
\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
is an uncountable Cantor–like set that accumulates to one from above and below (Remark 2.4
[
2
]
).

Remark 2.3

(Connection to Hurwitz’s theorem)
.

According to Hurwitz’s theorem, for every irrational number
\ssomega
there are infinitely many relatively prime integers
\ssomega_{j}
and
l
such that

\displaystyle\left|\ssomega\cdot l-\ssomega_{j}\right|<\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\frac{1}{l}

and moreover the constant
\sqrt{5}
is optimal. Consequently,
\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}=\emptyset
, for
\alpha\geq\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}
. In this note, we pick a suitable
\alpha
with
0<\alpha<1/3
.

The main result of
[
2
]
reads as follows.

Theorem 2.4

(Original version of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem
[
2
]
)
.

For
0<\alpha<1/3
, define
\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
according to (
2.3
) and consider the operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}\rangle(\xi).

Assume that conditions (
1
) and (
2
) are verified. Moreover, let
\xi_{0}
be such that

•

\xi_{0}
is a zero of
\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left(\xi_{0}\right)=0,

•

and
\xi_{0}
satisfies the following non–degeneracy condition

\displaystyle\ker\left(d\mathcal{M}\left(\xi_{0}\right)\right)=\{0\}.

Then, there exists a family
\{u_{\epsilon}:\epsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}\}
of time–periodic solutions to (
2.1
)–(
2.2
) where
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}
is an uncountable set that has zero as an accumulation point. In addition, each element
u_{\epsilon}
has the following properties:

(1)

u_{\epsilon}
has period
T_{\epsilon}=2\pi/\ssomega_{\epsilon}
and there exists
\ssomega_{\star}>0
such that the map
\epsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}\longmapsto\ssomega_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}\cap[1,1+\ssomega_{\star})
is a monotone, one-to-one map that stays close to one,
|1-\ssomega_{\epsilon}|\lesssim\epsilon^{r-1}
,

(2)

u_{\epsilon}\in H^{1}([0,T_{\epsilon}];l_{s}^{2})
,

(3)

u_{\epsilon}
stays for all times close to the solution to the linearized equation with initial data
\left(u_{t=0},\partial_{t}u_{t=0}\right)=\left(\epsilon\xi_{0},0\right)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot)-\pPhi^{t\ssomega_{\epsilon}}\left(\epsilon\xi_{0}\right)\right|_{s}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}.

2.2.
A modified Bambusi–Paleari theorem

As we will see in Section
4.3
, in the case of the YM model, the non–linearity is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}(u)=\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)+\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(u),

(2.4)

where

•

the lowest degree term
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
is a homogeneous polynomial of order
2
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m}

\displaystyle:=-3\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}u^{i}(t)u^{j}(t),

(2.5)

•

the highest degree term
\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}
is a homogeneous polynomial of order
3
,

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m}

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{i,j,k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}u^{i}(t)u^{j}(t)u^{k}(t),

(2.6)

Thus, according to the original version of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem (Theorem
2.4
), one may argue that
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
is the main non-linearity and
\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}
can be treated perturbatively. However, in this setting, the original version of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem would not be applicable, because
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
is
non–resonant
(Lemma
6.5
), that is

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\rangle(\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\pPhi^{t}\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right)dt=0,

(2.7)

for all initial data
\xi
, and therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{A}\xi

leading to trivial zeros of the operator
\mathcal{M}
. Consequently, we need to revisit the theorem of Bambusi–Paleari in this context. Specifically, we consider (
2.1
)–(
2.4
)–(
2.7
), replace
\mathfrak{f}^{(0)}(u)
by
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)+\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(u)
and establish the following theorem.

Theorem 2.5

(Modification of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem)
.

Let
0<\alpha<1/3
and define
\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
according to (
2.3
). Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a positive multiplication self–adjoint operator with pure point and resonant spectrum
\{\ssomega_{j}^{2}>0:j\geq 0\}
with
\ssomega_{j}\sim j
, as
j\rightarrow\infty
, defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}:\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})\simeq l_{s+2}^{2}\longrightarrow l_{s}^{2},\hskip 5.0pt(\mathfrak{A}u)^{j}:=\ssomega_{j}^{2}u_{j},

with
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})
being its maximal domain of definition. Assume that
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}+\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}
, where
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}
admit the representations (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) respectively. Moreover, assume that both
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}
are differentiable, with Lipschitz differentials and define the modified operator

\displaystyle{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\pm\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)+\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi),

where
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)=\{\left(\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)\right)^{m}:m\geq 0\}
is a bounded map on
l_{s}^{2}
that is given by

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)\right)^{m}=

\displaystyle\frac{9}{4}\sum_{\kappa,\nu\geq 0}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\kappa\nu m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\geq 0\\
\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{j}\neq\pm\ssomega_{\nu}\end{subarray}}\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ij\nu}}{\ssomega_{\nu}^{2}-(\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{j})^{2}}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\xi^{\kappa}\sum_{\pm}\mathds{1}(\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{j}\pm\ssomega_{\kappa}\pm\ssomega_{m}=0)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{9}{4}\sum_{\kappa,\nu\geq 0}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\kappa\nu m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\geq 0\\
\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}\neq\pm\ssomega_{\nu}\end{subarray}}\frac{\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ij\nu}}{\ssomega_{\nu}^{2}-(\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j})^{2}}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\xi^{\kappa}\sum_{\pm}\mathds{1}(\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}\pm\ssomega_{\kappa}\pm\ssomega_{m}=0).

Also, let
\xi_{0}\in l_{s+3}^{2}
be such that

•

\xi_{0}
is a zero of
{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}
,

\displaystyle{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}\left(\xi_{0}\right)=0,

•

and
\xi_{0}
satisfies the following non–degeneracy condition

\displaystyle\ker\left(d{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}\left(\xi_{0}\right)\right)=\{0\}.

Then, there exists a family
\{u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot):\epsilon\in\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}\}
of time–periodic solutions to (
2.1
)–(
2.4
)–(
2.7
) where
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha}
is an uncountable set that has zero as an accumulation point. In addition, each element
u_{\epsilon}
has the following properties:

(1)

u_{\epsilon}
has period
T_{\epsilon}=2\pi/\ssomega_{\epsilon}
where there exists
\ssomega_{\star}>0
such that the maps

•

\epsilon\longmapsto\ssomega_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}\cap[1,1+\ssomega_{\star})
, for
{\swabfamily M}_{+}

•

\epsilon\longmapsto\ssomega_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}\cap(1-\ssomega_{\star},1]
, for
{\swabfamily M}_{-}

are monotone, one-to-one map that stay close to one,
|1-\ssomega_{\epsilon}|\lesssim\epsilon
,

(2)

u_{\epsilon}\in H^{1}([0,T_{\epsilon}];l_{s}^{2}])
,

(3)

u_{\epsilon}
stays close to the solution to the linearized equation with the same initial data as above and zero initial velocity,

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left|u_{\epsilon}(t,\cdot)-\pPhi^{t\ssomega_{\epsilon}}\left(\epsilon\xi_{0}\right)\right|_{s}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of the theorem above.

2.3.
Preliminaries

The core of the proof follows that of
[
2
]
. Let
0<\alpha<1/3
and pick a frequency
\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
. We are looking for a solution to (
2.1
) with frequency
\ssomega
, that is

\displaystyle u(t):=q(\ssomega t).

(2.8)

For any integer
k\geq 0
, we define the Banach space

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}\subseteq H^{k}([0,2\pi];l_{s}^{2})

consisting of spacetime functions

\displaystyle q(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}q^{j}(t)e_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}q^{lj}\cos(lt)\right)e_{j}

such that the norm

\displaystyle\|q\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}^{2}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left(2|q^{0j}|^{2}+\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}|q^{lj}|^{2}(1+l^{2})^{k}\right)

is finite. In particular, we aim towards constructing
q
in the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}
. To do so, we substitute (
2.8
) into (
2.1
) and obtain the non–linear equation

\displaystyle L_{\ssomega}q=f(q),

(2.9)

where

\displaystyle L_{\ssomega}:\mathcal{D}(L_{\ssomega})\subset\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s},\hskip 5.0ptL_{\ssomega}q:=\ssomega^{2}\frac{d^{2}}{dt^{2}}q+\mathfrak{A}q.

Now, we are looking for a solution with frequency close to one. For this reason, we split
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}
into

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}=K\oplus R,\hskip 5.0ptK:=\ker(L_{1}),\hskip 5.0ptR:=K^{\perp},

and write

\displaystyle q\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s},\hskip 5.0ptq=v+q_{\perp},\hskip 5.0ptv\in K,\hskip 5.0ptq_{\perp}\in R.

Taking into account the fact that
K
is generated by
\{\cos(\ssomega_{j}t):j\geq 0\}
, since

\displaystyle v\in K\Longleftrightarrow v(t)=\{v^{j}(t)=c^{j}\cos(\ssomega_{j}t),\hskip 5.0ptj\geq 0\},

for some constants
c^{j}
, the latter simply means that we split
q=\{q^{j}:j\geq 0\}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}
into

\displaystyle q^{j}(t)=v^{j}(t)+q_{\perp}^{j}(t),\hskip 5.0ptv^{j}(t)=c^{j}\cos(\ssomega_{j}t),\hskip 5.0ptq_{\perp}^{j}(t)=\sum_{l\neq\ssomega_{j}}d^{jl}\cos(lt),

for some constants
c^{j}
and
d^{jl}
. In addition, we define the associated projections

\displaystyle P:\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}\longrightarrow R,\hskip 5.0ptP(q)=P(v+q_{\perp}):=q_{\perp},

\displaystyle Q:\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}\longrightarrow K,\hskip 5.0ptQ(q)=Q(v+q_{\perp}):=v,

and project (
2.9
) onto
R
and
K
respectively. We obtain the following coupled non–linear system

\displaystyle L_{\ssomega}q_{\perp}

\displaystyle=Pf(v+q_{\perp}),

(2.10)

\displaystyle-2\beta Av

\displaystyle=Qf(v+q_{\perp}),

(2.11)

where we also set

\displaystyle\ssomega^{2}=1+2\beta.

(2.12)

As is usual in this setting, we refer to (
2.10
) and (
2.11
) as the
P-
equation and
Q-
equation respectively.

2.4.
Solution to the P–equation

As we will now see, the Diophantine condition
\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
guarantees the existence of a solution to the
P-
equation.

Lemma 2.6

(Solution to the
P-
equation, Lemma 4.6 in
[
2
]
)
.

Let
0<\alpha<1/3
and pick
\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
. Then, the operator
L_{\ssomega}
restricted to
R
admits a bounded inverse

\displaystyle L_{\ssomega}^{-1}:\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}\cap R\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}\cap R

such that

\displaystyle\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}\|\leq c_{0}\alpha^{-1},

for some positive constant
c_{0}
. Moreover, there exists
\rho=\rho(\alpha)>0
and a
C^{1}-
function

\displaystyle q_{\perp}:B_{\rho}\longrightarrow R,\hskip 5.0ptv\longmapsto q_{\perp}(v)

that solves the
P-
equation, where
B_{\rho}
denotes the ball of radius
\rho
in
K
centered at zero. Furthermore, we have the estimates

\displaystyle\|q_{\perp}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2},\hskip 5.0pt\|q_{\perp}(v)-L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}.

Proof.

Apart from the
C^{1}
regularity of
q_{\perp}
(which is stated only as Lipschitz in
[
2
]
), the proof coincides with the one of Lemma 4.6 in
[
2
]
, where the
f^{(0)}
in
[
2
]
is replaced by
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
. Once a Lipschitz solution
q_{\perp}
has been found, one can read–off the
C^{1}
regularity of
q_{\perp}
based on the regularity of
\mathfrak{f}
. However, for the convenience of the reader, we give a proof below of the construction of
q_{\perp}
. Let
0<\alpha<1/3
and pick
\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
. The eigenvalues of
L_{\ssomega}
are given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{jl}=\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}\ssomega^{2}=(\ssomega_{j}-l\ssomega)(\ssomega_{j}+l\ssomega).

(2.13)

Then, for all
(l,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
with
l\geq 1
and
l\neq\ssomega_{j}
, we have

\displaystyle|\lambda_{jl}|\geq\frac{\alpha}{l}(\ssomega_{j}+l\ssomega)\geq\alpha\ssomega\geq\frac{\alpha}{2}.

Therefore,
L_{\ssomega}|_{R}
has a bounded inverse and there exists a positive constant
c_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}\|\leq c_{0}\alpha^{-1}.

In addition, we let
\epsilon>0
be sufficiently small,
\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\leq\epsilon
,
\delta>0
sufficiently large, define the closed ball of radius
\delta\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}
centered at
L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)
, that is

\displaystyle B:=\{w\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}:\hskip 5.0pt\|w-L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\leq\delta\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}\},

and rewrite the the
P-
equation in the fixed point formulation as follows

\displaystyle q_{\perp}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{F}(q_{\perp}):=L_{\ssomega}^{-1}\left[P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)+P\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v+q_{\perp})-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\right)+P\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(v+q_{\perp})\right].

Next, we show that
\mathcal{F}
maps the closed ball to itself. Indeed, for all
w\in B
, we have

\displaystyle\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\|w-L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}+\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\delta\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}+\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}\|\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\delta\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}+c_{0}\alpha^{-1}k_{s}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}

and Lemma
4.5
implies

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v+w)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq k_{s}\left(\|v+w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}+\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\right)\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq k_{s}\left(\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}+2\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\right)\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}k_{s}\left(c_{1}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}+2\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\right)\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3},

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(v+w)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq k_{s}\|v+w\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim k_{s}\left(\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}+c_{1}^{3}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{6}\right)

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}.

Hence, we infer

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}(w)-L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle=\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}\left[P\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v+w)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\right)+P\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(v+w)\right]\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}

\displaystyle\leq\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}\|\left[\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v+w)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}+\|\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(v+w)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}\alpha^{-1}\left[c_{2}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}+c_{3}\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\delta\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3},

by choosing
\delta
sufficiently large. The contraction property follows similarly. For the
C^{1}
regularity, we set
\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}:=L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
and, for
v,v+h\in B_{\rho}
, we have

\displaystyle q_{\perp}(v)=\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}(v+q_{\perp}(v)),\hskip 5.0ptq_{\perp}(v+h)=\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}(v+h+q_{\perp}(v+h)),

so that

\displaystyle q_{\perp}(v+h)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}(v+q_{\perp}(v))+d\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{v+q_{\perp}(v)}\left(h+q_{\perp}(v+h)-q_{\perp}(v)\right)+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(h+q_{\perp}(v+h)-q_{\perp}(v))

\displaystyle=q_{\perp}(v)+d\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{v+q_{\perp}(v)}\left(h+q_{\perp}(v+h)-q_{\perp}(v)\right)+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(h),

using that
q_{\perp}
is Lipschitz. Assuming that
\rho
is small enough, we can ensure that
\|d\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{v+q_{\perp}(v)}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\leq c\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}<1-\delta
, uniformly in
v
, and hence

\displaystyle q_{\perp}(v+h)=q_{\perp}(v)+(\text{Id}-d\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{v+q_{\perp}(v)})^{-1}d\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{v+q_{\perp}(v)}\left(h\right)+\mathchoice{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}}}{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{O}$}}(h),

so that
q_{\perp}(v)
is
C^{1}
with differential
(\text{Id}-d\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{v+q_{\perp}(v)})^{-1}d\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}_{v+q_{\perp}(v)}
.

∎

2.5.
Solution to the Q–equation

Next, we turn our attention to the existence of a solution to the
Q-
equation. Firstly, we define the following two Banach spaces of initial data,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}:=\left\{\xi=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\xi^{j}e_{j}:\hskip 5.0pt\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}<\infty\right\}\simeq l_{s+1}^{2}\subseteq l_{s}^{2},

endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left|\xi^{j}\right|^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left|\ssomega_{j}\xi^{j}\right|^{2}\simeq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2(s+1)}\left|\xi^{j}\right|^{2}=|\xi|^{2}_{s+1},

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A}):=\left\{\xi=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\xi^{j}e_{j}:\hskip 5.0pt\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})}^{2}<\infty\right\}\simeq l_{s+2}^{2}\subseteq l_{s}^{2},

endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})}^{2}:=|\xi|_{s}^{2}+|\mathfrak{A}\xi|_{s}^{2}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left|\xi^{j}\right|^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left|\ssomega_{j}^{2}\xi^{j}\right|^{2}\simeq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2(s+2)}\left|\xi^{j}\right|^{2}=|\xi|^{2}_{s+2},

since
\ssomega_{j}\sim j
, as
j\rightarrow\infty
. We call the Hilbert space
(\mathcal{Q},\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{Q}})
the configuration space. In fact,
\mathcal{Q}
is isomorphic to
K:=\ker(L_{1})
and the isomorphism is given by the linear flow

\displaystyle I:\mathcal{Q}\longrightarrow K,\hskip 5.0pt\hskip 5.0pt(I(x))(t):=\pPhi^{t}(x).

Also, recall the Banach space the Banach space of spacetime functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}:=\left\{q(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}q^{j}(t)e_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}q^{lj}\cos(lt)\right)e_{j}:\hskip 5.0pt\|q\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}^{2}<\infty\right\}\subseteq H^{k}([0,2\pi];l_{s}^{2})

endowed with the norm

\displaystyle\|q\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}^{2}:=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left(2|q^{0j}|^{2}+\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}|q^{lj}|^{2}(1+l^{2})^{k}\right).

Notice that, since

\displaystyle I(\xi)(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(I(\xi)(t))^{j}e_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\Phi^{t}(\xi))^{j}e_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\xi^{j}\cos(\ssomega_{j}t)e_{j}

and
\ssomega_{j}\neq 0
, for all integers
j\geq 0
, we have that

\displaystyle\|I(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}|\xi^{j}|^{2}=|\xi|_{s}^{2},

\displaystyle\|I(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}|\xi^{j}|^{2}(1+\ssomega_{j}^{2})=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left(|\xi^{j}|^{2}+|\ssomega_{j}\xi^{j}|^{2}\right)\simeq|\xi|_{s+1}^{2}\simeq\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\|I(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{2}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}|\xi^{j}|^{2}(1+\ssomega_{j}^{2})^{2}\simeq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\left(|\xi^{j}|^{2}+|\ssomega_{j}^{2}\xi^{j}|^{2}\right)\simeq|\xi|_{s+2}^{2}\simeq\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{A})}^{2}.

(2.15)

Secondly, we prove the following averaging identity that generalizes the one in Lemma 4.7 in Bambusi–Paleari
[
2
]
from vector fields
F:\mathcal{Q}\rightarrow\mathcal{Q}
to
F:\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}
.

Lemma 2.7

(Averaging Identity)
.

Let
F:\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}
be any vector field. Then, for all
x\in l_{s}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle F\rangle(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi^{t}\left[F\left(w\right)\right]dt=\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[F(I(x))\right].

Proof.

Let
F:\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}
be a vector field in
\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}
(not necessarily in
\mathcal{Q}
), pick any
x\in l_{s}^{2}
and set
w=I(x)
. By the definition of the Banach space
\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}
, we have

\displaystyle F\left(w\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(F\left(w\right)\right)^{m}e_{m}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left(F\left(w\right)\right)_{l}^{m}\cos(lt)\right)e_{m},

where

\displaystyle\left(F\left(w\right)\right)_{l}^{m}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(F\left(w\right)\right)^{m}\cos(lt)dt.

Then, the definition of the linear flow together with the definition of the projection
Q
yield

\displaystyle Q\left[F\left(w\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(Q\left[F\left(w\right)\right]\right)^{m}e_{m}=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(F\left(w\right)\right)_{\omega_{m}}^{m}\cos(\ssomega_{m}t)e_{m},

\displaystyle I^{-1}Q\left[F\left(w\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(F\left(w\right)\right)_{\ssomega_{m}}^{m}e_{m},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[F\left(w\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(F\left(w\right)\right)_{\ssomega_{m}}^{m}e_{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(F\left(w\right)\right)^{m}\cos(\ssomega_{m}t)dt\right)e_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(F\left(w\right)\right)^{m}\cos(\ssomega_{m}t)dt\right)e_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\left(\Phi^{t}\left[F\left(w\right)\right]\right)^{m}dt\right)e_{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\left(\Phi^{t}\left[F\left(w\right)\right]\right)^{m}e_{m}\right)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi^{t}\left[F\left(w\right)\right]dt:=\langle F\rangle(x),

that competes the proof.
∎

Then, we express the
Q-
equation in the configuration space introduced above.

Lemma 2.8

(The
Q-
equation in the configuration space)
.

Let
\rho>0
and
q_{\perp}:B_{\rho}\subset K\rightarrow R
be the solution map to the P–equation derived in Lemma
2.6
. Also, let
x\in l_{s+2}^{2}
and set
v=I(x)\in B_{\rho}
. Then, the
Q-
equation (
2.11
) for
v
is equivalent to

\displaystyle\beta\mathfrak{A}x+\langle\mathfrak{f}\rangle(x)=-\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}\left(I(x)+q_{\perp}(I(x))\right)-\mathfrak{f}\left(I(x)\right)\right].

(2.16)

Proof.

Let
\rho>0
and
q_{\perp}:B_{\rho}\subset K\rightarrow R
be the solution map to the
P-
equation derived in Lemma
2.6
. Also, let
x\in\mathcal{Q}
and set
v=I(x)\in B_{\rho}
. Then, we rewrite the
Q-
equation given in (
2.11
), that is

\displaystyle-2\beta Av=Qf(v+q_{\perp}),

as follows

\displaystyle-2\beta\mathfrak{A}I(x)-Q\mathfrak{f}(I(x))=Q\mathfrak{f}(I(x)+q_{\perp}(I(x)))-Q\mathfrak{f}(I(x)).

Since
\mathfrak{A}I(x)=I\mathfrak{A}x
, the latter is equivalent to

\displaystyle-2\beta I\mathfrak{A}x-Q\mathfrak{f}(I(x))=Q\left[\mathfrak{f}(I(x)+q_{\perp}(I(x)))-\mathfrak{f}(I(x))\right]

and, by applying
-(1/2)I^{-1}
to both sides, we get

\displaystyle\beta\mathfrak{A}x+\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\mathfrak{f}I(x))=-\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}(I(x)+q_{\perp}(I(x)))-\mathfrak{f}(I(x))\right].

Now, the claim follows by the averaging identity due to Lemma
2.7
.
∎

It remains to show that there exists a solution to (
2.16
). To this end, we define

\displaystyle x=\epsilon\xi,\hskip 5.0pt|\beta|=\epsilon^{2}

(2.17)

and (
2.16
) becomes

\displaystyle\pm\epsilon^{2}\mathfrak{A}(\epsilon\xi)+\langle\mathfrak{f}\rangle(\epsilon\xi)=-\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)\right)\right].

On the one hand, (
2.4
) and (
2.7
) yield

\displaystyle\pm\epsilon^{2}\mathfrak{A}(\epsilon\xi)+\langle\mathfrak{f}\rangle(\epsilon\xi)=\pm\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{A}\xi+\epsilon^{2}\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\rangle(\xi)+\epsilon^{3}\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)=\epsilon^{3}\left(\pm\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)\right).

On the other hand, (
2.4
), (
2.7
) and the averaging identity from Lemma
2.7
yield

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)\right)\right]+\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]-\epsilon^{2}\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\rangle(\xi)+\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]+\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]+\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi),

where we set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)

\displaystyle:=\epsilon^{-3}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right]\Bigg].

In addition, we apply the averaging identity and the notation from Lemma
2.7
to the map
v\rightarrow\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(v+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\right)
that is a vector field from
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}
to
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]+\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]+\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)+\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi^{-t}\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(\Phi^{t}(\epsilon\xi)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(\Phi^{t}(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right)dt+\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)+\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)=

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left((\cdot)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(\cdot)\right)\rangle(\epsilon\xi)+\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)+\epsilon^{3}\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{3}\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi)+\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)+\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)\right),

where we set

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)

\displaystyle:=\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right],

\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi)

\displaystyle:=\epsilon^{-3}\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left((\cdot)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(\cdot)\right)\rangle(\epsilon\xi).

In conclusion, the Q–equation (
2.16
) can be written equivalently, for
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small, as

\displaystyle\pm\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)=-\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi)+\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)+\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)\right).

(2.18)

However, instead of (
2.18
), we focus on a modified version, namely

\displaystyle\pm\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)=-\left(\mathfrak{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi)+\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)\pm\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{\ssomega^{2}-1}\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)\right).

(2.19)

Notice that (
2.18
) coincides with (
2.19
) provided that
\ssomega^{2}-1=\pm 2\epsilon^{2}
.

Remark 2.9
.

Since
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
is differentiable, quadratic and
\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\rangle(\xi)=0
, for all initial data
\xi\in l_{s+3}^{2}
, it follows that
\mathfrak{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi)
is differentiable and
\|\mathfrak{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim\epsilon
. Later, (Section
2.6
), we compute the exact expressions of
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)
for general initial data (Lemma
2.13
),

\mathfrak{F}_{\epsilon}(\xi)
for small
\epsilon
close to zero and 1–mode initial data (Lemma
2.14
),
as well as the differential
d\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)
at the 1–mode initial data (Lemma
2.15
).

In the following, we estimate the error terms. To begin with, we estimate
\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)
.

Lemma 2.10

(Estimate for
\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)
and
d_{\xi}\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)
)
.

Let
0<\alpha<1/3
and pick any
\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
. Also, let
\xi\in l_{s+3}^{2}
be any initial data. Then, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim|\ssomega^{2}-1|,\hskip 5.0pt\|d_{\xi}\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim|\ssomega^{2}-1|.

Proof.

Let
0<\alpha<1/3
and pick any
\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
. Also, let
\xi\in l_{s+3}^{2}
be any initial data. Firstly, we pick any
\epsilon>0
, set
v=I(\epsilon\xi)
and compute

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v))^{j}e_{j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v))_{l}^{j}\cos(lt)\right)e_{j},

\displaystyle P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=0\\
l\neq\ssomega_{j}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v))_{l}^{j}\cos(lt)\right)e_{j},

\displaystyle L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=0\\
l\neq\ssomega_{j}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}\ssomega^{2}}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v))_{l}^{j}\cos(lt)\right)e_{j},

\displaystyle\left(L_{\ssomega}^{-1}-L_{1}^{-1}\right)P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=0\\
l\neq\ssomega_{j}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}\ssomega^{2}}-\frac{1}{\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}}\right)(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v))_{l}^{j}\cos(lt)\right)e_{j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l=0\\
l\neq\ssomega_{j}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{l^{2}(\ssomega^{2}-1)}{(\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}\ssomega^{2})(\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2})}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v))_{l}^{j}\cos(lt)\right)e_{j}.

Secondly, we note that

\displaystyle\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\epsilon^{2},\hskip 5.0pt\|L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}.

(2.20)

These can be easily proved by using the Diophantine condition, the elementary inequality
|\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}|\geq 1
(since
\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
, both
\ssomega_{j}^{2}\geq 1
and
l^{2}\geq 0
are integers with
\ssomega_{j}\neq l
), the Lipschitz estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\lesssim_{s}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}^{2},

for all
u\in\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}
with
\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\leq\epsilon
that follows from Lemma
4.5
, together with (
2.14
). Indeed, we infer

\displaystyle\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left|\frac{1}{\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}\ssomega^{2}}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}(1+l^{2})

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left|(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}(1+l^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|I(\epsilon\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{4}=\epsilon^{4}\|I(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{4}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{4}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{Q}}^{4}\leq\epsilon^{4}|\xi|_{s+1}^{4}\leq\epsilon^{4}|\xi|_{s+3}^{4},

\displaystyle\|L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left|\frac{1}{\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}(1+l^{2})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\left|(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}(1+l^{2})

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim\|I(\epsilon\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{4}=\epsilon^{4}\|I(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{4}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{4}\|\xi\|_{\mathcal{Q}}^{4}\leq\epsilon^{4}|\xi|_{s+1}^{4}\leq\epsilon^{4}|\xi|_{s+3}^{4}.

Next, we use the above together with the Lipschitz estimate for
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
(Lemma
4.5
) and the fact that
I^{-1}:\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))\right]-\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))\right]\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}}=

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))\right]\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\left\|I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}+\left\|I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\right)\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\left\|L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))-L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\epsilon\left\|\left(L_{\ssomega}^{-1}-L_{1}^{-1}\right)P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}.

Once again, the Diophantine condition, the elementary inequality
|\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}|\geq 1
, the Lipschitz estimate

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\lesssim_{s}\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}^{2},

for all
u\in\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}
with
\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\leq\epsilon
that follows from Lemma
4.5
, together with (
2.14
), imply that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(L_{\ssomega}^{-1}-L_{1}^{-1}\right)P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle=|\ssomega^{2}-1|^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\left|\frac{l^{2}}{(\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2}\ssomega^{2})(\ssomega_{j}^{2}-l^{2})}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}(1+l^{2})

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}|\ssomega^{2}-1|^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\left|l^{2}(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}(1+l^{2})

\displaystyle\lesssim|\ssomega^{2}-1|^{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{2s}\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\left|(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi)))_{l}^{j}\right|^{2}(1+l^{2})^{3}

\displaystyle\leq|\ssomega^{2}-1|^{2}\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\|_{\mathcal{H}^{3}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim|\ssomega^{2}-1|^{2}\|I(\epsilon\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{3}_{s}}^{4}

\displaystyle=|\ssomega^{2}-1|^{2}\epsilon^{4}\|I(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{3}_{s}}^{4}\lesssim|\ssomega^{2}-1|^{2}\epsilon^{4}|\xi|_{s+3}^{2}

which in turn yields

\displaystyle\left\|\left(L_{\ssomega}^{-1}-L_{1}^{-1}\right)P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim|\ssomega^{2}-1|\epsilon^{2},

due to (
2.12
) and (
2.17
) respectively. Finally, putting all together yields

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{R}_{\epsilon}(\xi,\ssomega)\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}}=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\left\|\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{1}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]\rangle(\epsilon\xi)\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\epsilon\left\|\left(L_{\ssomega}^{-1}-L_{1}^{-1}\right)P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\epsilon^{-3}\epsilon|\ssomega^{2}-1|\epsilon^{2}=|\ssomega^{2}-1|,

that completes the proof. The estimate for the differential follows similarly.
∎

Next, we estimate
\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)
and its differential.

Lemma 2.11

(Estimate for
\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)
and
d_{\xi}\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)
)
.

Let
\xi\in l_{s+3}^{2}
be any initial data. Then,
\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)
is continuously differentiable with respect to
\xi
and we have

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim\epsilon,\hskip 5.0pt\|d_{\xi}\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim\epsilon.

Proof.

Let
\xi\in l_{s+3}^{2}
be any initial data and recall that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\epsilon}(\xi)

\displaystyle:=\epsilon^{-3}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right]\Bigg].

The claim follows by Lemmata
4.5
and
2.6
together with (
2.14
) and the fact that
I^{-1}:\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}
. Indeed, since

\displaystyle\left\|I(\epsilon\xi)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\epsilon\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}},\hskip 5.0pt\left\|q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\left\|I(\epsilon\xi)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}\left\|\xi\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}}^{2},

(2.21)

we can estimate

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right]\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon\left\|q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))-L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\epsilon\left\|I(\epsilon\xi)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{3}\lesssim\epsilon^{4}

and

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right]\right\|_{\mathcal{Q}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left\|\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)-\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left[\left\|I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}+\left\|I(\epsilon\xi)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}\right]\left\|q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left[\left\|I(\epsilon\xi)\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}+\left\|q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}^{2}\right]\left\|q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{s}}\lesssim\epsilon^{4}.

The estimate for the differential follows similarly. Indeed, we pick any
\xi\in l_{s+3}^{2}
and
\overline{\xi}=\xi+\overline{\epsilon}h\in l_{s+3}^{2}
for some
\overline{\epsilon}>0
and
h\in l_{s+3}^{2}
. We have that

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi})+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi}))\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi+\overline{\epsilon}\cdot\epsilon h)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi+\overline{\epsilon}\cdot\epsilon h))\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+\overline{\epsilon}I(\epsilon h)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi)+\overline{\epsilon}I(\epsilon h))\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+\overline{\epsilon}I(\epsilon h)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))+\overline{\epsilon}(dq_{\perp})_{I(\epsilon\xi)}[I(\epsilon h)]+\mathcal{O}(\overline{\epsilon}^{2})\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))+\overline{\epsilon}\left(I(\epsilon h)+(dq_{\perp})_{I(\epsilon\xi)}[I(\epsilon h)]\right)+\mathcal{O}(\overline{\epsilon}^{2})\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)+\overline{\epsilon}(d\mathfrak{f}^{(2)})_{I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))}\left[I(\epsilon h)+(dq_{\perp})_{I(\epsilon\xi)}[I(\epsilon h)]\right]+\mathcal{O}(\overline{\epsilon}^{2})\right].

Similarly, we set
\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}:=L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
and obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi})+L_{\ssomega}^{-1}P\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi}))\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi})+\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi}))\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)+\overline{\epsilon}(d\mathfrak{f}^{(2)})_{I(\epsilon\xi)+\mathfrak{F}^{(2)}(I(\epsilon\xi))}\left[I(\epsilon h)+(d\mathfrak{F}^{(2)})_{I(\epsilon\xi)}[I(\epsilon h)]\right]+\mathcal{O}(\overline{\epsilon}^{2})\right]

and

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi})+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi}))\right)\right]=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))\right)+\overline{\epsilon}(d\mathfrak{f}^{(3)})_{I(\epsilon\xi)+q_{\perp}(I(\epsilon\xi))}\left[I(\epsilon h)+(dq_{\perp})_{I(\epsilon\xi)}[I(\epsilon h)]\right]+\mathcal{O}(\overline{\epsilon}^{2})\right]

as well as

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-3}\frac{1}{2}I^{-1}Q\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(I(\epsilon\overline{\xi}))\right]=
