is at least
l_{3}^{2}
(Theorem
2.4
).

Proof.

The proof coincides with the one of Lemma
4.1
.
∎

Finally, we consider the Yang–Mills equation in spherical symmetry.

Lemma 4.5

(Lipschitz bounds for the YM)
.

Let
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}
be given by (
4.9
) and (
4.10
) respectivel. Then, for all integers
k\geq 0
and
s\geq 3
, there exists a positive constant (depending only on
k
and
s
) such that

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)-\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}+\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\right)\|u-v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}},

\displaystyle\left\|d\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)[h]-d\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)[h]\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\left\|u-v\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}},

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(u)-\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(v)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}^{2}+\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}^{2}\right)\|u-v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}},

\displaystyle\left\|d\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)[h]-d\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(v)[h]\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\left\|u\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}+\left\|v\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\right)\left\|h\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\left\|u-v\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}},

for all
u,v,h\in\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}
with
\|u\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\leq\epsilon
,
\|v\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\leq\epsilon
and
\|h\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{s}}\leq\epsilon
.

Remark 4.6

(Regularity of the initial data for the YM model)
.

As stated above, for the YM model, we require
s\in\mathbb{N}
with
s\geq 3
. This means that the space of initial data
l_{s+3}^{2}
is at least
l_{6}^{2}
(Lemma
2.10
, Theorem
2.5
).

Proof.

Let
s\geq 3
be an integer and pick any
u=\{u^{j}:j\geq 0\}\in l^{2}_{s}
. We also denote by
u
the corresponding function in the physical space, that is

\displaystyle u(x):=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}u_{j}\mathfrak{e}_{j}(x)

and recall that the operator in physical space is given by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}u:=-\frac{1}{\sin^{4}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(\sin^{4}(x)\partial_{x}u\right)+4u.

In the following, we claim that the operator

\displaystyle\Delta_{\text{YM}}u:=\frac{1}{\sin^{4}(x)}\partial_{x}\left(\sin^{4}(x)\partial_{x}u\right)

coincides with the Laplace–Beltrami operator
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}u
on the sphere
\mathbb{S}^{5}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{6}
restricted to a class of symmetric functions. Indeed, we endow
\mathbb{S}^{5}
with the round metric and consider the standard Eulerian coordinates
\left(x_{1}=x,x_{2},x_{3},x_{4},x_{5}\right)\in(0,\pi)^{4}\times(0,2\pi)
, so that
y=(y^{1},y^{2},y^{3},y^{4},y^{5},y^{6})\in\mathbb{S}^{5}
with

\displaystyle y^{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos x_{1},

\displaystyle y^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sin x_{1}\cos x_{2},

\displaystyle y^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sin x_{1}\sin x_{2}\cos x_{3},

\displaystyle y^{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sin x_{1}\sin x_{2}\sin x_{3}\cos x_{4},

\displaystyle y^{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sin x_{1}\sin x_{2}\sin x_{3}\sin x_{4}\cos x_{5},

\displaystyle y^{6}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sin x_{1}\sin x_{2}\sin x_{3}\sin x_{4}\sin x_{5}.

The metric element in these coordinates is given by the standard round metric on
\mathbb{S}^{5}
. Then, for a function
u
defined on
\mathbb{S}^{5}
that is invariant under all rotations around the
y^{6}-
axis, the operator
\Delta_{\text{YM}}u
coincides with
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}u
. We denote such functions on
\mathbb{S}^{5}
below as spherically-symmetric. On the one hand, for any integer
s\geq 3
, we define the Sobolev space
H^{s}_{\text{YM}}
for spherically symmetric functions as follows

\displaystyle\|u\|^{2}_{H^{k}_{\text{YM}}}:=\int_{0}^{\pi}u\mathfrak{L}^{k}u\sin^{4}xdx=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\ssomega_{j}^{2k}|u_{j}|^{2}\simeq|u|^{2}_{k},

since
\ssomega_{j}\simeq j
. On the other hand, note that, with a slight abuse of notation (we denote by
u
the original variable as well as the spherically symmetric version of it), we have that the Sobolev space above is equivalent to the standrad Sobolev space on
\mathbb{S}^{5}
, defined by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H^{s}\left(\mathbb{S}^{5}\right)}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}u(-\Delta^{s}_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}u)d\text{vol}_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}+\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{5})},

that is

\displaystyle\|q\|_{H^{s}_{\text{YM}}}^{2}\simeq\|q\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{5})}^{2}.

Here,
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}
stands for the standard Laplacian on
\mathbb{S}^{5}
for the round metric and the standard volume form
dvol_{\mathbb{S}^{5}}
. This equivalence, yields that
H^{s}_{\text{YM}}
is an algebra, since
H^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{5})
is an algebra provided that
s\geq 5/2
. We pick an integer
s\geq 3
and the rest of the proof coincides with the one of Lemma
4.1
.
∎

5.
The Fourier coefficients

This section is devoted to the study of the Fourier coefficients, as defined by (
4.1
), (
4.4
) and (
4.7
). Since the eigenfunctions are given by Jacobi polynomials and since the Fourier coefficients involve products of the eigenfunctions, these are a priori complicated integrals, depending on the indices of the eigenfunctions. Nonetheless, we will derive here explicit closed formulas for the various Fourier coefficients on resonant indices.

5.1.
Conformal cubic wave equation in spherical symmetry

In this case, the Fourier coefficients are given by (
4.1
), that is

\displaystyle C_{ijkm}:

\displaystyle=(e_{i}e_{j}e_{k}|e_{m})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}e_{i}(x)e_{j}(x)e_{k}(x)e_{m}(x)\sin^{2}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}U_{i}(y)U_{j}(y)U_{k}(y)U_{m}(y)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}dy,

where we used the definition of the Chebyshev polynomials of the second kind,

\displaystyle e_{n}(x):=U_{n}(\cos(x))=\frac{\sin(\omega_{n}x)}{\sin(x)},

for all
n\in\{i,j,k,m\}
. Next, we call
resonant
a quadruple
(i,j,k,m)
of indices such that

\displaystyle\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{k}\pm\omega_{m}=0

and study the Fourier coefficients on resonant indices.

5.1.1.
Vanishing Fourier coefficients

Firstly, we show that the Fourier coefficients vanish on some resonant indices.

Lemma 5.1

(Vanishing Fourier coefficients on resonant indices)
.

For any integers
i,j,k,m\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{k}-\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow C_{ijkm}=0,

\displaystyle\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}+\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow C_{ijkm}=0,

\displaystyle\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{k}+\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow C_{ijkm}=0,

\displaystyle-\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{k}+\omega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow C_{ijkm}=0.

Proof.

Let
i,j,k,m
be positive integers such that
\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}+\omega_{k}-\omega_{m}=0
. Then,
m=2+i+j+k
and according to the computation above, we have

\displaystyle C_{ijkm}=\int_{-1}^{1}R_{N}(y)U_{m}(y)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}dy,

where

\displaystyle R_{N}(y):=\frac{2}{\pi}U_{i}(y)U_{j}(y)U_{k}(y)

is a polynomial of degree
N=i+j+k<m
and hence the Fourier coefficient vanishes since
U_{m}(y)
forms an orthonormal and complete basis with respect to the weight
\sqrt{1-y^{2}}
. The other results now follow immediately using the symmetries of the Fourier coefficients with respect to
i,j,k,m
.
∎

5.1.2.
Non–vanishing Fourier coefficients

Secondly, we study the non–vanishing Fourier coefficients on resonant indices. In order to deal with these constants, one needs a computationally efficient formula. In the spherically symmetric case, where the basis consists of the Chebyshev polynomials, there exists the addition formula
[
38
]

\displaystyle U_{p}(y)U_{q}(y)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r=|q-p|\\
\text{step 2}\end{subarray}}^{p+q}U_{r}(y)=\sum_{s=0}^{\min(p,q)}U_{|q-p|+2s}(y),

(5.1)

for all
p,q\geq 0
. Consequently, one obtains the closed formula

\displaystyle C_{ijkm}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r=|j-i|\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{j+i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=|m-k|\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{m+k}\mathds{1}\left(r=s\right),

(5.2)

for any integers
i,j,k,m\geq 0
. Indeed, we implement the addition formula above together with the orthogonality property for the Chebyshev polynomials with respect to the weight
\sqrt{1-y^{2}}
to obtain

\displaystyle C_{ijkm}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}U_{i}(y)U_{j}(y)U_{k}(y)U_{m}(y)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r=|j-i|\\
\text{step 2}\end{subarray}}^{i+j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=|m-k|\\
\text{step 2}\end{subarray}}^{m+k}\int_{-1}^{1}U_{r}(y)U_{s}(y)\sqrt{1-y^{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r=|j-i|\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{j+i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=|m-k|\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{m+k}\mathds{1}\left(r=s\right).

Next, we use (
5.2
) to derive closed formulas for the non-vanishing Fourier coefficients.

Lemma 5.2

(Non–vanishing Fourier coefficients on resonant indices: closed formulas)
.

For any integers
i,j,k,m\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}-\omega_{m}=0\Longrightarrow C_{ijkm}=\omega_{\min\{i,j,k,m\}},

\displaystyle\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}+\omega_{k}-\omega_{m}=0\Longrightarrow C_{ijkm}=\omega_{\min\{i,j,k,m\}},

\displaystyle\omega_{i}-\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}+\omega_{m}=0\Longrightarrow C_{ijkm}=\omega_{\min\{i,j,k,m\}}.

Proof.

Let
i,j,k,m\geq 0
be integers such that
\omega_{i}+\omega_{j}-\omega_{k}-\omega_{m}=0
or equivalently,
m=i+j-k
and assume for simplicity that
i\leq j\leq k\leq m
. Then, (
5.2
) yields

\displaystyle C_{ijkm}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r=j-i\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{j+i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s=i+j-2k\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{i+j}\mathds{1}\left(r=s\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{2i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{2k}\mathds{1}\left(i+j-p=i+j-q\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=0\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{2i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q=0\\
\text{step }2\end{subarray}}^{2k}\mathds{1}\left(p=q\right)=\sum_{t=0}^{i}\sum_{\tau=0}^{k}\mathds{1}\left(t=\tau\right)=\sum_{t=0}^{i}\left(\sum_{\tau=0}^{i}\mathds{1}\left(t=\tau\right)+\sum_{\tau=i+1}^{k}\mathds{1}\left(t=\tau\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{i}\sum_{\tau=0}^{i}\mathds{1}\left(t=\tau\right)=i+1=\omega_{i}.

All the other results follow immediately by the symmetries of the Fourier coefficients with respect to
i,j,k
and
m
, that completes the proof.
∎

5.2.
Conformal cubic wave equation out of spherical symmetry

In this case, the Fourier coefficients are given by (
4.4
), that is

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{ijkm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}:

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathsf{e}_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\mathsf{e}_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\mathsf{e}_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\Big|\mathsf{e}_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\mathsf{e}_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)\mathsf{e}_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)\mathsf{e}_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)\mathsf{e}_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)\sin(2x)dx,

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{\lambda_{1}\in\{i,j,k,m\}}\mathsf{N}_{\lambda_{1}}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\int_{-1}^{1}(1-x)^{2\mu_{1}}(1+x)^{2\mu_{2}}\prod_{\lambda_{2}\in\{i,j,k,m\}}P_{\lambda_{2}}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)dx,

where the normalization constant
\mathsf{N}_{\lambda_{1}}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}
is given by (
3.5
). As before, we call
resonant
a quadruple
(i,j,k,m)
of indices such that

\displaystyle\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0

and study the Fourier coefficients on resonant indices.

5.2.1.
Vanishing Fourier coefficients

Firstly, we show that the Fourier coefficients vanish on some resonant indices.

Lemma 5.3

(Vanishing Fourier coefficients on resonant indices)
.

For any integers
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\geq 0
and
i,j,k,m\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\mathsf{C}_{ijkm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,

\displaystyle\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\mathsf{C}_{ijkm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,

\displaystyle\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\mathsf{C}_{ijkm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,

\displaystyle-\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\mathsf{C}_{ijkm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0.

Proof.

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\geq 0
and
i,j,k,m\geq 0
be integers such that
\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0
. Then,
m=i+j+k+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+1
and according to the computation above, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{ijkm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=\int_{-1}^{1}\mathsf{R}_{N}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)P_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)(1-x)^{\mu_{1}}(1+x)^{\mu_{2}}dx,

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}_{N}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x):=\frac{1}{2}\prod_{\lambda_{1}\in\{i,j,k,m\}}\mathsf{N}_{\lambda_{1}}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(1-x)^{\mu_{1}}(1+x)^{\mu_{2}}\prod_{\lambda_{2}\in\{i,j,k\}}P_{\lambda_{2}}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)

is a polynomial of degree
N=i+j+k+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}<m
and hence the Fourier coefficient vanishes since
P_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)
forms an orthonormal and complete basis with respect to the weight
(1-x)^{\mu_{1}}(1+x)^{\mu_{2}}
. The other results now follow immediately using the symmetries of the Fourier coefficients with respect to
i,j,k,m
.
∎

5.2.2.
Non–vanishing Fourier coefficients

Next, we study the non–vanishing Fourier coefficients. In principle, in order to deal with these constants, one needs a computationally efficient formula as in the spherically symmetric case. However, out of spherically symmetry, where the basis consists of the Jacobi polynomials, although there exists the addition formula

\displaystyle P_{p}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)P_{q}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x)=\sum_{r=|p-q|}^{p+q}L(p,q,r)P_{r}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(x),

for all
p,q\geq 0
, similar to (
5.1
), the linearization coefficients
L(p,q,r)
remain unknown in closed form for generic values of
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
and hence a closed formula similar to (
5.2
) is not available in general. We note that Rahman
8
8

8

According to
[
20
]
, there was a minor typo in Rahman’s published result; in the linearization coefficient in
[
35
]
, the term
(-\alpha-\beta-2m)
should be replaced by the Pochhammer symbol
(-\alpha-\beta-2m)_{k}
. The corrected linearization formula is given in
[
20
]
.

(page 919 in
[
35
]
) was able to prove that the linearization coefficients of Jacobi polynomials can be represented as a well–posed hypergeometric function
{}_{9}F_{8}(1)
. On the other hand, in the special case where
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}
, the Jacobi polynomials reduce to Gegenbauer polynomials for which the linearization coefficients are given by well-posed and closed formulas
[
31
,
36
,
38
]
. Hence, we restrict ourselves to the case

\displaystyle\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}:=\mu

and also denote by
\gamma
the index referring to the fixed choice of the 1–mode initial data. Then, we derive closed formulas for the non–vanishing Fourier coefficients for a resonant pair of indices
(i,j,k,m)
by using the formula (equation (20) in
[
36
]
) for the Gegenbauer polynomials,

\displaystyle\left(C_{m}^{\left(\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\lambda=0}^{m}L_{\mu m}(\lambda)C_{2\lambda}^{\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x),

(5.3)

where the coefficients are given by

\displaystyle L_{\mu m}(\lambda):=\frac{(2\mu+1)_{m}}{\Gamma(m+1)}\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)_{\lambda}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)_{m-\lambda}\left(\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)_{\lambda}(\lambda+2\mu+1)_{m}}{\Gamma(m-\lambda+1)(\mu+1)_{\lambda}\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)_{2\lambda}\left(2\lambda+2\mu+\frac{3}{2}\right)_{m-\lambda}},

(5.4)

valid of any real
x\in[-1,1]
and integers
\mu,m,\lambda\geq 0
and

\displaystyle(a)_{n}:=\frac{\Gamma(a+n)}{\Gamma(a)},

stands for the Pochhammer’s symbol defined for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
with
a\notin\{0,-1,-2,\dots\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Notice that (
5.3
) is a combination of a linearization and a connection formula for Gegenbauer polynomials. Specifically, we establish the following result.

Lemma 5.4

(Non–vanishing Fourier coefficients on resonant indices: closed formulas)
.

Let
\gamma\geq 0
and
m\geq\gamma
be any integers. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu,\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\gamma}\mathsf{M}_{\gamma}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)\xi_{\lambda}(\mu),

where

\displaystyle\xi_{\lambda}(\mu):=\frac{\pi 2^{1-4\mu}\Gamma(2\lambda+4\mu+1)}{(4\lambda+4\mu+1)\Gamma(2\lambda+1)\left(\Gamma\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)^{2}}

and

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{3/2}}\frac{(4\lambda+4\mu+1)\Gamma\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\lambda+\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\lambda+\mu+1)\Gamma(\lambda+2\mu+1)}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{(2\mu+2m+1)\Gamma\left(m-\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(m+\lambda+2\mu+1)}{\Gamma(m-\lambda+1)\Gamma\left(m+\lambda+2\mu+\frac{3}{2}\right)}.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\geq 1
be a fixed integer and pick any integer
m\geq\gamma
. Then, by the definition of the Fourier coefficient, the fact that the Jacobi polynomials with equal parameters can be written in terms of the Gegenbauer polynomials (equation 18.7.1 in
[
31
]
),

\displaystyle P_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}(x)=w_{m}^{(\mu)}C_{m}^{\left(\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x),\hskip 5.0ptw_{m}^{(\mu)}:=\frac{\Gamma(2\mu+1)\Gamma(m+\mu+1)}{\Gamma(\mu+1)\Gamma(m+2\mu+1)},

together with (
5.3
)–(
5.4
), we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu,\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathsf{N}_{\gamma}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\left(\mathsf{N}_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}(1-x)^{2\mu}(1+x)^{2\mu}\left(P_{\gamma}^{(\mu,\mu)}(x)\right)^{2}\left(P_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}(x)\right)^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(w_{\gamma}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{\gamma}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\left(w_{m}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}(1-x^{2})^{2\mu}\left(C_{\gamma}^{\left(\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x)\right)^{2}\left(C_{m}^{\left(\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x)\right)^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(w_{\gamma}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{\gamma}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\left(w_{m}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\gamma}L_{\mu\gamma}(\nu)\sum_{\lambda=0}^{m}L_{\mu m}(\lambda)\int_{-1}^{1}(1-x^{2})^{2\mu}C_{2\nu}^{\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x)C_{2\lambda}^{\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x)dx.

Now, the orthogonality of the Gegenbauer polynomials,

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}C_{2\nu}^{\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x)C_{2\lambda}^{\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}(x)(1-x^{2})^{2\mu}dx=\xi_{\lambda}(\mu)\mathds{1}(\nu=\lambda),

where

\displaystyle\xi_{\lambda}(\mu):=\frac{\pi 2^{1-4\mu}\Gamma(2\lambda+4\mu+1)}{(4\lambda+4\mu+1)\Gamma(2\lambda+1)\left(\Gamma\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)^{2}},

together with the fact that
0\leq\gamma\leq m
, yields

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu,\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(w_{\gamma}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{\gamma}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\left(w_{m}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\gamma}L_{\mu\gamma}(\nu)\sum_{\lambda=0}^{m}L_{\mu m}(\lambda)\xi_{\lambda}(\mu)\mathds{1}(\nu=\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(w_{\gamma}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{\gamma}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\left(w_{m}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\sum_{\nu=0}^{\gamma}L_{\mu\gamma}(\nu)\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\gamma}L_{\mu m}(\lambda)\xi_{\lambda}(\mu)\mathds{1}(\nu=\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(w_{\gamma}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{\gamma}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\left(w_{m}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\gamma}L_{\mu\gamma}(\lambda)L_{\mu m}(\lambda)\xi_{\lambda}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\lambda=0}^{\gamma}\mathsf{M}_{\gamma}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)\xi_{\lambda}(\mu),

where we set

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\left(w_{m}^{(\mu)}\mathsf{N}_{m}^{(\mu,\mu)}\right)^{2}L_{\mu m}(\lambda).

Finally, a direct computation yields that

\displaystyle\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi^{3/2}}\frac{(4\lambda+4\mu+1)\Gamma\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\lambda+\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\lambda+\mu+1)\Gamma(\lambda+2\mu+1)}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{(2\mu+2m+1)\Gamma\left(m-\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(m+\lambda+2\mu+1)}{\Gamma(m-\lambda+1)\Gamma\left(m+\lambda+2\mu+\frac{3}{2}\right)}

that completes the proof.
∎

Next, we show that the closed formulas we derived above are in fact monotone with respect to
m
.

Lemma 5.5

(Monotonicity of
\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)
)
.

Let
\gamma\geq 0
,
m\geq\gamma
and
0\leq\lambda\leq\gamma
be any integers. Then, the function
\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)
defined in Lemma
5.4
is decreasing with respect to
m
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\geq 0
,
m\geq\gamma
and
0\leq\lambda\leq\gamma
be any integers. The claim follows immediately by computing the difference
\mathsf{M}_{m+1}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)-\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)
. Indeed, the identity for the ration of two Gamma functions,
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)
, valid for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, yields that
\mathsf{M}_{m+1}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)-\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)
equals to

\displaystyle-\frac{(4\lambda+4\mu+1)\Gamma\left(\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(2\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\lambda+\mu+\frac{3}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(m-\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(m+\lambda+2\mu+1)}{\pi^{3/2}\Gamma(\lambda+\mu)\Gamma(\lambda+2\mu+1)\Gamma(m-\lambda+2)\Gamma\left(m+\lambda+2\mu+\frac{5}{2}\right)}

that is strictly negative for all
m
,
\lambda
and
\mu
, and hence
\mathsf{M}_{m}^{(\mu)}(\lambda)
is decreasing with respect to
m
, for all
\lambda
and
\mu
, that completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.6

(Closed formulas for
\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu,\mu)}
for small values of
\gamma
)
.

Finally, we note that one can use Lemma
5.4
to find closed formulas for the Fourier coefficients provided that
\gamma
is sufficiently small. For example, for
\gamma\in\{0,1\}
, we find that

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{00mm}^{(\mu,\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}(2\mu+1)\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\mu+1)}\right)^{2}\frac{(2\mu+2m+1)\Gamma\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(m+2\mu+1)}{\Gamma(m+1)\Gamma\left(m+2\mu+\frac{3}{2}\right)},

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{11mm}^{(\mu,\mu)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8\pi}(\mu+1)(2\mu+1)(2\mu+3)\left(\frac{\Gamma\left(\mu+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\mu+2)}\right)^{2}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\frac{(2\mu+2m+1)(-\mu+2m(2\mu+m+1)-1)\Gamma\left(m-\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(m+2\mu+1)}{\Gamma(m+1)\Gamma\left(m+2\mu+\frac{5}{2}\right)}.

Figure
1
illustrates the Fourier coefficients
\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu,\mu)}
for
\mu=30
and
\gamma\in\{0,1,2\}
respectively as
m
varies within
\{1,2,\dots,50\}
.

Figure 1.
The Fourier coefficients
\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(30,30)}
for
\gamma=0
(blue),
\gamma=1
(orange) and
\gamma=2
(green) as
m
varies within the interval
[1,50]
. They are decreasing for
m\geq 2\gamma+1
.

5.3.
Yang–Mills equation in spherical symmetry

In this case, the Fourier coefficients are given by (
4.7
), that is

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}

\displaystyle:=\left[\mathfrak{e}_{i}\mathfrak{e}_{j}\Big|\mathfrak{e}_{m}\right]=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathfrak{e}_{i}(x)\mathfrak{e}_{j}(x)\mathfrak{e}_{m}(x)\sin^{4}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\prod_{\lambda_{1}\in\{i,j,m\}}\mathfrak{N}_{\lambda_{1}}\int_{-1}^{1}\prod_{\lambda_{2}\in\{i,j,m\}}P_{\lambda_{2}}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{2}\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1-y^{2}}},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}

\displaystyle:=\left[\sin^{2}\cdot\mathfrak{e}_{i}\mathfrak{e}_{j}\mathfrak{e}_{k}\Big|\mathfrak{e}_{m}\right]=\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathfrak{e}_{i}(x)\mathfrak{e}_{j}(x)\mathfrak{e}_{k}(x)\mathfrak{e}_{m}(x)\sin^{6}(x)dx

\displaystyle=\prod_{\lambda_{1}\in\{i,j,k,m\}}\mathfrak{N}_{\lambda_{1}}\int_{-1}^{1}\prod_{\lambda_{2}\in\{i,j,k,m\}}P_{\lambda_{2}}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{3}\frac{dy}{\sqrt{1-y^{2}}},

where the normalization constant
\mathfrak{N}_{\lambda_{1}}
is given by (
3.8
). As before, we call
resonant
a quadruple
(i,j,k,m)
of indices such that

\displaystyle\ssomega_{i}\pm\ssomega_{j}\pm\ssomega_{k}\pm\ssomega_{m}=0

and study the Fourier coefficients on resonant indices.

5.3.1.
Vanishing Fourier coefficients

Firstly, we show that the Fourier coefficients vanish on some resonant indices.

Lemma 5.7

(Vanishing Fourier coefficients on resonant indices)
.

For any integers
i,j,k,m\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}-\ssomega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}=0,

\displaystyle\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}=0,

\displaystyle-\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}=0

and

\displaystyle\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{k}-\ssomega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}=0,

\displaystyle\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}-\ssomega_{k}+\ssomega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}=0,

\displaystyle\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{k}+\ssomega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}=0,

\displaystyle-\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{k}+\ssomega_{m}

\displaystyle=0\Longrightarrow\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}=0.

Proof.

Let
i,j,k,m\geq 0
be integers such that
\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{k}-\ssomega_{m}=0
. Then,
m=2+i+j
and according to the computation above, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}=\int_{-1}^{1}\overline{\mathfrak{R}}_{N}(y)P_{m}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(y)(1-y^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}dy,

where

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{R}}_{N}(y):=\prod_{\lambda_{1}\in\{i,j,m\}}\mathfrak{N}_{\lambda_{1}}\prod_{\lambda_{2}\in\{i,j\}}P_{\lambda_{2}}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(y)

is a polynomial of degree
N=i+j<m
and hence the Fourier coefficient vanishes since
P_{m}^{\left(3/2,3/2\right)}(y)
forms an orthonormal and complete basis with respect to the weight
(1-y^{2})^{3/2}
. The other results now follow immediately using the symmetries of the Fourier coefficients with respect to
i,j,m
. The second result follows similarly. Let
i,j,k,m\geq 0
be integers such that
\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{k}-\ssomega_{m}=0
. Then,
m=i+j+k+4
and according to the definition of the Fourier coefficients above, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}=\int_{-1}^{1}\mathfrak{R}_{N}(y)P_{m}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(y)(1-y^{2})^{\frac{3}{2}}dy,

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{N}(y):=(1-y^{2})\prod_{\lambda_{1}\in\{i,j,k,m\}}\mathfrak{N}_{\lambda_{1}}\prod_{\lambda_{1}\in\{i,j,k\}}P_{\lambda_{2}}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(y)

is a polynomial of degree
N=i+j+k+2<m
and hence the Fourier coefficient vanishes since
P_{m}^{\left(3/2,3/2\right)}(y)
forms an orthonormal and complete basis with respect to the weight
(1-y^{2})^{3/2}
. The other results now follow immediately using the symmetries of the Fourier coefficients with respect to
i,j,k,m
.
∎

5.3.2.
Non–vanishing Fourier coefficients

Next, we study the non–vanishing Fourier coefficients. In order to deal with these constants, one needs a computationally efficient formula as in the two previous cases. In the spherically symmetric case we consider here, the basis consists of the Jacobi polynomials with equal parameters and these are weighted Gegenbauer polynomials (equation 18.7.1 in
[
31
]
)

\displaystyle C_{n}^{(2)}(x):=\frac{(4)_{n}}{\left(\frac{5}{2}\right)_{n}}P_{n}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(x)=\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{5}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(4\right)}\frac{\Gamma\left(n+4\right)}{\Gamma\left(n+\frac{5}{2}\right)}P_{n}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(x)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{8}\frac{\Gamma\left(n+4\right)}{\Gamma\left(n+\frac{5}{2}\right)}P_{n}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(x),

for
n\in\{\gamma,m\}
and
x\in[-1,1]
. The latter, together with the definition of the normalization constant
\mathfrak{N}_{n}
from (
3.8
), expresses the normalized Jacobi polynomials in terms of the normalized Gegenbauer polynomials as follows

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{n}P_{n}^{\left(\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)}(x)=\mathfrak{N}_{n}\frac{8}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma\left(n+\frac{5}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(n+4\right)}C_{n}^{(2)}(x)=\mathfrak{w}_{n}C_{n}^{(2)}(x),

for all
n\in\{\gamma,m\}
, where the new normalization constant reads

\displaystyle\mathfrak{w}_{n}:=\sqrt{\frac{8}{\pi}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{(n+1)(n+3)}}.

Consequently, the Fourier coefficients can be written in terms of the Gegenbauer polynomials as follows

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}_{i}\mathfrak{w}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{m}\int_{-1}^{1}C_{i}^{(2)}(y)C_{j}^{(2)}(y)C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}dy,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}_{i}\mathfrak{w}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{k}\mathfrak{w}_{m}\int_{-1}^{1}C_{i}^{(2)}(y)C_{j}^{(2)}(y)C_{k}^{(2)}(y)C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy.

Then, we derive closed formulas for the non–vanishing Fourier coefficients for a resonant quadruple
(i,j,k,m)
as follows:

•

Whenever a Gegenbauer polynomial has an order
\mu
such that the weight
(1-y^{2})^{\mu-1/2}
(with respect to which it forms an orthonormal basis) does not coincide with the weights
(1-y^{2})^{p}
with
p\in\{3/2,5/2\}
(that define the integrals above), we use the connection formula (18.18.16 in
[
31
]
)

\displaystyle C^{(\mu)}_{n}\left(y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\left\lfloor n/2\right\rfloor}\alpha_{n\mu\lambda}(\ell)C^{(\lambda)}_{n-2\ell}\left(y\right),

(5.5)

where the coefficients are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{n\mu\lambda}(\ell):=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda+n-2\ell}{\lambda}\frac{{\left(\mu\right)_{n-\ell}}}{{\left(\lambda+1\right)_{n-\ell}}}\frac{{\left(\mu-\lambda\right)_{\ell}}}{\ell!}.

(5.6)

In particular, we are going to use this only for
\lambda=\mu+1
. In this case, the latter is equivalent to the recurrence relation (18.9.7 in
[
31
]
)

C^{(\mu)}_{n}\left(x\right)=\frac{\mu}{n+\mu}\left(C^{(\mu+1)}_{n}\left(x\right)-C^{(\mu+1)}_{n-2}\left(x\right)\right),

valid for all integers
\mu\geq 0
and
n\geq 2
.

•

Whenever a Gegenbauer polynomial is multiplied by itself, we use the addition formula (equation (19) in
[
36
]
and 18.18.22 in
[
31
]
)

\displaystyle\left(C^{(\lambda)}_{n}\left(y\right)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\beta_{n\lambda}(\ell)C^{(\lambda)}_{2\ell}\left(y\right),

(5.7)

where the coefficients are given by

\displaystyle\beta_{n\lambda}(\ell):=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n!}\binom{n}{\ell}\frac{(2\ell)!(\lambda)_{\ell}(\lambda)_{n-\ell}(2\ell+2\lambda)_{n-\ell}}{\ell!(\ell+\lambda)_{\ell}(2\ell+\lambda+1)_{n-\ell}}.

(5.8)

•

Whenever two Gegenbauer polynomials of different degrees but of same order are multiplied, we use the addition formula (18.18.22 in
[
31
]
)

\displaystyle C^{(\lambda)}_{m}\left(y\right)C^{(\lambda)}_{n}\left(y\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\min(m,n)}\zeta_{mn\lambda}(\ell)C^{(\lambda)}_{m+n-2\ell}\left(y\right),

(5.9)

where the coefficients are given by

\displaystyle\zeta_{mn\lambda}(\ell):=

\displaystyle\frac{(m+n+\lambda-2\ell)(m+n-2\ell)!}{(m+n+\lambda-\ell)\ell!\,(m-\ell)!\,(n-\ell)!}\*\frac{{\left(\lambda\right)_{\ell}}{\left(\lambda\right)_{m-\ell}}{\left(\lambda\right)_{n-\ell}}{\left(2\lambda\right)_{m+n-\ell}}}{{\left(\lambda\right)_{m+n-\ell}}{\left(2\lambda\right)_{m+n-2\ell}}}.

(5.10)

•

Whenever a product of a monomial with a Gegenbauer polynomial is integrated, we use the formula (equation 18.17.37 in
[
31
]
)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}x^{z-1}C^{(\lambda)}_{n}\left(x\right)(1-x^{2})^{\lambda-\frac{1}{2}}dx=\frac{\pi\,2^{1-2\lambda-z}\Gamma\left(n+2\lambda\right)\Gamma\left(z\right)}{n!\Gamma\left(\lambda\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}n+\lambda+\frac{1}{2}z\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}z-\frac{1}{2}n\right)},

(5.11)

valid of all integers
\lambda\geq 0
and real numbers
z>0
. Notice that this is the Mellin transform of the function
C^{(\lambda)}_{n}\left(x\right)(1-x^{2})^{\lambda-\frac{1}{2}}
restricted to
[0,1]
.

In particular, we will only need to study

•

\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\gamma,\gamma,2\tau}
, for all integers ,
\tau\in\{0,1,\dots,\gamma\}
,

•

\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\gamma,2\tau+m-\gamma,m}
, for all integers
\tau\in\{0,1,\dots,\gamma\}
,
m\geq 2\gamma+1
,

•

\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{m,2\tau,m}
, for all integers
\tau\in\{0,1,\dots,\gamma\}
,
m\geq 2\gamma+1
,

•

\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}
for all integers
m\geq 2\gamma+1
.

To begin with, we focus on
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}
for any integers
i,j,m\geq 0
and establish the follow result.

Lemma 5.8

(Non–vanishing Fourier coefficients
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}
on resonant indices: closed formula)
.

For any integers
i,j,m\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}

\displaystyle=\frac{(i+j-m+2)(i-j+m+2)(-i+j+m+2)(i+j+m+6)}{4\sqrt{2\pi(i+1)(i+3)(j+1)(j+3)(m+1)(m+3)}}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\mathds{1}\left(|j-m|\leq i\leq j+m\right)\mathds{1}\left(|i-m|\leq j\leq i+m\right)\mathds{1}\left(|i-j|\leq m\leq i+j\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\mathds{1}(j+m-i\in 2\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})\mathds{1}(i+m-j\in 2\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})\mathds{1}(i+j-m\in 2\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}).

Proof.

The result follows immediately from (
5.9
)–(
5.10
) together with the orthogonality of the Gegenbauer polynomials,

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}C^{(2)}_{n}\left(y\right)C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}dy=\frac{\pi}{8}(m+1)(m+3)\mathds{1}(m=n),

for all integers
m,n\geq 0
. Indeed, for any integers
i,j,m\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}_{i}\mathfrak{w}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{m}\int_{-1}^{1}C_{i}^{(2)}(y)C_{j}^{(2)}(y)C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}_{i}\mathfrak{w}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{m}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\min(i,j)}\zeta_{ij2}(\ell)\int_{-1}^{1}C^{(2)}_{i+j-2\ell}\left(y\right)C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{8}(m+1)(m+3)\mathfrak{w}_{i}\mathfrak{w}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{m}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\min(i,j)}\zeta_{ij2}(\ell)\mathds{1}(2\ell=i+j-m).

On the one hand, for all integers
i
,
j
and
m
such that
i+j-m\notin 2\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, the Fourier coefficient vanishes. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle 0\leq\frac{i+j-m}{2}\leq\min(i,j)\Longleftrightarrow|i-j|\leq m\leq i+j.

Consequently, for all integers
i
,
j
and
m
such that the condition
|i-j|\leq m\leq i+j
is not fulfilled, the Fourier coefficient vanishes. On the other hand, for all
i
,
j
and
m
such that both
i+j-m\in 2\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and
|i-j|\leq m\leq i+j
hold true, we compute

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{8}(m+1)(m+3)\mathfrak{w}_{i}\mathfrak{w}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{m}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\min(i,j)}\zeta_{ij2}(\ell)\mathds{1}(2\ell=i+j-m)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{8}(m+1)(m+3)\mathfrak{w}_{i}\mathfrak{w}_{j}\mathfrak{w}_{m}\zeta_{ij2}\left(\frac{i+j-m}{2}\right)\sum_{\ell=0}^{\min(i,j)}\mathds{1}(2\ell=i+j-m)

\displaystyle=\frac{(i+j-m+2)(i-j+m+2)(-i+j+m+2)(i+j+m+6)}{4\sqrt{2\pi(i+1)(i+3)(j+1)(j+3)(m+1)(m+3)}}\sum_{l=0}^{\min(i,j)}\mathds{1}(i+j-m=2\ell)

\displaystyle=\frac{(i+j-m+2)(i-j+m+2)(-i+j+m+2)(i+j+m+6)}{4\sqrt{2\pi(i+1)(i+3)(j+1)(j+3)(m+1)(m+3)}},

where we used the fact that

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\min(i,j)}\mathds{1}(i+j-m=2\ell)=1,

for all such
i
,
j
and
m
. Finally, using the symmetries of the Fourier coefficient with respect to
i
,
j
and
m
completes the proof.
∎

Next, we apply the previous result to obtain closed formulas for the Fourier coefficient
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}
on the particular resonant indices we are interested in. Specifically, we establish the following result.

Lemma 5.9

(Non–vanishing Fourier coefficients
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}
on particular resonant indices: closed formulas)
.

Let
\gamma,\tau,m
be integers such that
\gamma\geq 0
,
\tau\in\{0,1,\dots,\gamma\}
and
m\geq 2\gamma+1
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\gamma,\gamma,2\tau}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{(\tau+1)^{2}(\gamma-\tau+1)(\gamma+\tau+3)}{(\gamma+1)(\gamma+3)\sqrt{4\tau(\tau+2)+3}},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{m,2\tau,m}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{(\tau+1)^{2}(m-\tau+1)(m+\tau+3)}{(m+1)(m+3)\sqrt{4\tau(\tau+2)+3}},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\gamma,2\tau+m-\gamma,m}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\frac{(\tau+1)(\gamma-\tau+1)(m+\tau+3)(-\gamma+m+\tau+1)}{\sqrt{(\gamma+1)(\gamma+3)(m+1)(m+3)(-\gamma+m+2\tau+1)(-\gamma+m+2\tau+3)}}.

Proof.

Let
\gamma,\tau,m
be integers such that
\gamma\geq 0
,
\tau\in\{0,1,\dots,\gamma\}
and
m\geq 2\gamma+1
. Firstly, notice that all the indices of the Fourier coefficients above satisfy all the conditions in the Booleans in Lemma
5.8
. Then, the result follows immediately by Lemma
5.8
by direct substitution.
∎

Now, we focus on
\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}
and derive the following result.

Lemma 5.10

(Non–vanishing Fourier coefficients
\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}
on resonant indices: closed formulas)
.

Let
\gamma\geq 0
be a fixed integer. Then, for all
m\geq 2\gamma+1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}=\mathfrak{w}_{\gamma}^{2}\mathfrak{w}_{m}^{2}\sum_{\ell_{2}=0}^{\gamma}\sum_{\nu_{2}=0}^{\ell_{2}}\delta_{\gamma}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})J_{m}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})

where

\displaystyle\delta_{\gamma}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})

\displaystyle:=\frac{(\ell_{2}+1)^{2}(-1)^{\nu_{2}}(\gamma-\ell_{2}+1)(\gamma+\ell_{2}+3)2^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}\Gamma(2\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+2)}{(4\ell_{2}(\ell_{2}+2)+3)\Gamma(\nu_{2}+1)\Gamma(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1)},

\displaystyle J_{m}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{3\sqrt{\pi}(5\ell_{2}(3m(m+4)+1)+m(m+4)(4-15\nu_{2})-5(\nu_{2}-4))\Gamma\left(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{8\Gamma(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+5)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1}\frac{\pi\ell_{1}\left(\ell_{1}+1\right)^{2}\left(2\ell_{1}-1\right)4^{-\ell_{2}+\nu_{2}-2}\left(\ell_{1}-m-1\right)\left(\ell_{1}+m+3\right)\Gamma\left(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+2\right)\Gamma\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+3\right)}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}}\frac{\pi\left(\ell_{1}+1\right)^{2}\left(\ell_{1}+2\right)\left(2\ell_{1}+5\right)4^{-\ell_{2}+\nu_{2}-2}\left(\ell_{1}-m-1\right)\left(\ell_{1}+m+3\right)\Gamma\left(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1\right)\Gamma\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+4\right)}.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\geq 0
be a fixed integer and pick any integer
m\geq 2\gamma+1
. Then, by the definition of the Fourier coefficients, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}_{\gamma}^{2}\mathfrak{w}_{m}^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\left(C_{\gamma}^{(2)}(y)\right)^{2}\left(C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\right)^{2}\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy.

On the one hand, we use the linearization formula (
5.7
)–(
5.8
) together with the special cases
C_{0}^{(2)}(y)=1
and
C_{2}^{(2)}(y)=12y^{2}-2
to obtain

\displaystyle\left(C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{m}\beta_{m2}(\ell_{1})C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(2)}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{1}=0}^{m}\frac{\left(\ell_{1}+1\right)^{2}\left(-\ell_{1}+m+1\right)\left(\ell_{1}+m+3\right)}{4\ell_{1}\left(\ell_{1}+2\right)+3}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(2)}(y),

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(m+1)(m+3)+\frac{4}{15}m(m+4)\left(12y^{2}-2\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{m}\frac{\left(\ell_{1}+1\right)^{2}\left(-\ell_{1}+m+1\right)\left(\ell_{1}+m+3\right)}{4\ell_{1}\left(\ell_{1}+2\right)+3}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(2)}(y).

Furthermore, for all
\ell_{1}\geq 2
, the connection formula (
5.5
)–(
5.6
) yields

\displaystyle C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(2)}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu_{1}=0}^{\ell_{1}}\alpha_{2\ell_{1},2,3}(\nu_{1})C_{2(\ell_{1}-\nu_{1})}^{(3)}(y)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\nu_{1}=0}^{\ell_{1}}\frac{(2\ell_{1}-2\nu_{1}+3)(-1)_{\nu_{1}}\Gamma(2\ell_{1}-\nu_{1}+2)}{3\nu_{1}!(4)_{2\ell_{1}-\nu_{1}}}C_{2(\ell_{1}-\nu_{1})}^{(3)}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\ell_{1}+3)(2)_{2\ell_{1}}}{3(4)_{2\ell_{1}}}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)-\frac{(2\ell_{1}+1)(2)_{2\ell_{1}-1}}{3(4)_{2\ell_{1}-1}}C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\ell_{1}+1}\left(C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)-C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)\right),

since
(-1)_{\nu_{1}}=0
for all
\nu_{1}\geq 2
. Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\left(C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(m+1)(m+3)+\frac{4}{15}m(m+4)\left(12y^{2}-2\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{m}\frac{(\ell_{1}+1)(\ell_{1}-m-1)(\ell_{1}+m+3)}{4\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+2)+3}\left(C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)-C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)\right).

On the other hand, the linearization formula (
5.7
)–(
5.8
) together with the definition of the Gegenbauer polynomials,

\displaystyle C_{2\ell_{2}}^{(2)}(y)=\sum_{\nu_{2}=0}^{\ell_{2}}d_{\ell_{2}}(\nu_{2})y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})},\hskip 5.0ptd_{\ell_{2}}(\nu_{2}):=\frac{(-1)^{\nu_{2}}\left(4^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}}\Gamma(2\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+2)\right)}{\Gamma(\nu_{2}+1)\Gamma(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1)}

yield

\displaystyle\left(C_{\gamma}^{(2)}(y)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{2}=0}^{\gamma}\beta_{\gamma 2}(\ell_{2})C_{2\ell_{2}}^{(2)}(y)=\sum_{\ell_{2}=0}^{\gamma}\beta_{\gamma 2}(\ell_{2})\sum_{\nu_{2}=0}^{\ell_{2}}d_{\ell_{2}}(\nu_{2})y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}=\sum_{\ell_{2}=0}^{\gamma}\sum_{\nu_{2}=0}^{\ell_{2}}\delta_{\gamma}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})},

where we set

\displaystyle\delta_{\gamma}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2}):=\beta_{\gamma 2}(\ell_{2})d_{\ell_{2}}(\nu_{2})=\frac{(\ell_{2}+1)^{2}(-1)^{\nu_{2}}(\gamma-\ell_{2}+1)(\gamma+\ell_{2}+3)2^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}\Gamma(2\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+2)}{(4\ell_{2}(\ell_{2}+2)+3)\Gamma(\nu_{2}+1)\Gamma(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1)}.

Now, putting all together, we infer

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}_{\gamma}^{2}\mathfrak{w}_{m}^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\left(C_{\gamma}^{(2)}(y)\right)^{2}\left(C_{m}^{(2)}(y)\right)^{2}\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}_{\gamma}^{2}\mathfrak{w}_{m}^{2}\sum_{\ell_{2}=0}^{\gamma}\sum_{\nu_{2}=0}^{\ell_{2}}\delta_{\gamma}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}\Bigg[\frac{1}{3}(m+1)(m+3)+\frac{4}{15}m(m+4)\left(12y^{2}-2\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{m}\frac{(\ell_{1}+1)(\ell_{1}-m-1)(\ell_{1}+m+3)}{4\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+2)+3}\left(C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)-C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)\right)\Bigg]\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{w}_{\gamma}^{2}\mathfrak{w}_{m}^{2}\sum_{\ell_{2}=0}^{\gamma}\sum_{\nu_{2}=0}^{\ell_{2}}\delta_{\gamma}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})J_{m}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2}),

where

\displaystyle J_{m}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{3}(m+1)(m+3)\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{15}m(m+4)\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}\left(12y^{2}-2\right)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{m}\frac{(\ell_{1}+1)(\ell_{1}-m-1)(\ell_{1}+m+3)}{4\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+2)+3}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle-\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{m}\frac{(\ell_{1}+1)(\ell_{1}-m-1)(\ell_{1}+m+3)}{4\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+2)+3}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy.

We compute

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{15\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma\left(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{8\Gamma(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+4)},

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}\left(12y^{2}-2\right)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{15\sqrt{\pi}(5\ell_{2}-5\nu_{2}-1)\Gamma\left(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{4\Gamma(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+5)}.

On the one hand, for all
\ell_{1}\geq 2
with
\ell_{1}>\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1
, we have
2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})<2(\ell_{1}-1)
and hence

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy=0

since the Gegenbauer polynomial in the integrand forms an orthonormal and complete basis with respect to the weight
(1-y^{2})^{5/2}
. On the other hand, for all
2\leq\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1
, the identity

\displaystyle C_{2\lambda}^{\left(3\right)}(-y)=C_{2\lambda}^{\left(3\right)}(y),

valid for all real
y\in[-1,1]
and integers
\lambda\geq 0
, yields

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy=2\int_{0}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi 4^{-\ell_{2}+\nu_{2}-3}\Gamma\left(2\ell_{1}+4\right)\Gamma\left(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(2\ell_{1}-1\right)\Gamma\left(-\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+2\right)\Gamma\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+3\right)},

where we used (
5.11
) to compute the last integral. In other words, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{m}\frac{(\ell_{1}+1)(\ell_{1}-m-1)(\ell_{1}+m+3)}{4\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+2)+3}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1}\frac{(\ell_{1}+1)(\ell_{1}-m-1)(\ell_{1}+m+3)}{4\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+2)+3}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2(\ell_{1}-1)}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1}\frac{\pi\ell_{1}\left(\ell_{1}+1\right)^{2}\left(2\ell_{1}-1\right)4^{-\ell_{2}+\nu_{2}-2}\left(\ell_{1}-m-1\right)\left(\ell_{1}+m+3\right)\Gamma\left(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+2\right)\Gamma\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+3\right)}.

Similarly, on the one hand, for all
\ell_{1}\geq 2
with
\ell_{1}>\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}
, we have
2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})<2\ell_{1}
and hence

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy=0

since the Gegenbauer polynomial in the integrand forms an orthonormal and complete basis with respect to the weight
(1-y^{2})^{5/2}
. On the other hand, for all
2\leq\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}
, the identity

\displaystyle C_{2\lambda}^{\left(3\right)}(-y)=C_{2\lambda}^{\left(3\right)}(y),

valid for all real
y\in[-1,1]
and integers
\lambda\geq 0
, yields

\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy=2\int_{0}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi 4^{-\ell_{2}+\nu_{2}-3}\Gamma\left(2\ell_{1}+6\right)\Gamma\left(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(2\ell_{1}+1\right)\Gamma\left(-\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1\right)\Gamma\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+4\right)},

where we used once again (
5.11
) to compute the last integral. In other words, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{m}\frac{(\ell_{1}+1)(\ell_{1}-m-1)(\ell_{1}+m+3)}{4\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+2)+3}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}}\frac{(\ell_{1}+1)(\ell_{1}-m-1)(\ell_{1}+m+3)}{4\ell_{1}(\ell_{1}+2)+3}\int_{-1}^{1}y^{2(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2})}C_{2\ell_{1}}^{(3)}(y)\left(1-y^{2}\right)^{\frac{5}{2}}dy=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}}\frac{\pi\left(\ell_{1}+1\right)^{2}\left(\ell_{1}+2\right)\left(2\ell_{1}+5\right)4^{-\ell_{2}+\nu_{2}-2}\left(\ell_{1}-m-1\right)\left(\ell_{1}+m+3\right)\Gamma\left(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1\right)\Gamma\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+4\right)}.

Putting all together, yields

\displaystyle J_{m}(\ell_{2},\nu_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{3\sqrt{\pi}(5\ell_{2}(3m(m+4)+1)+m(m+4)(4-15\nu_{2})-5(\nu_{2}-4))\Gamma\left(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{8\Gamma(\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+5)}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1}\frac{\pi\ell_{1}\left(\ell_{1}+1\right)^{2}\left(2\ell_{1}-1\right)4^{-\ell_{2}+\nu_{2}-2}\left(\ell_{1}-m-1\right)\left(\ell_{1}+m+3\right)\Gamma\left(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+2\right)\Gamma\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+3\right)}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\ell_{1}=2}^{\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}}\frac{\pi\left(\ell_{1}+1\right)^{2}\left(\ell_{1}+2\right)\left(2\ell_{1}+5\right)4^{-\ell_{2}+\nu_{2}-2}\left(\ell_{1}-m-1\right)\left(\ell_{1}+m+3\right)\Gamma\left(2\ell_{2}-2\nu_{2}+1\right)}{\Gamma\left(-\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+1\right)\Gamma\left(\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}-\nu_{2}+4\right)}

that completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.11

(Closed formulas for
\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}
for small values of
\gamma
)
.

Finally, we note that one can use Lemma
5.10
to find closed formulas for the Fourier coefficients provided that
\gamma
is sufficiently small. For example, for
\gamma\in\{0,1,2,3,4,5\}
, we find that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{00mm}

\displaystyle=\frac{4(m(m+4)+5)}{3\pi(m+1)(m+3)},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{11mm}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(m(m+4)+7)}{\pi(m+1)(m+3)},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{22mm}

\displaystyle=\frac{8(5m(m+4)+49)}{15\pi(m+1)(m+3)},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{33mm}

\displaystyle=\frac{2(5m(m+4)+67)}{3\pi(m+1)(m+3)},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{44mm}

\displaystyle=\frac{4(5m(m+4)+89)}{5\pi(m+1)(m+3)},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{55mm}

\displaystyle=\frac{14(m(m+4)+23)}{3\pi(m+1)(m+3)},

for all
m\geq 2\gamma+1
. Figure
2
illustrates the Fourier coefficients
\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}
for
\gamma\in\{0,1,2\}
respectively as
m
varies within
\{1,2,\dots,50\}
.

Figure 2.
The Fourier coefficients
\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}
for
\gamma=0
(blue),
\gamma=1
(orange) and
\gamma=2
(green) as
m
varies within
\{1,2,\dots,50\}
. They are decreasing for
m\geq 2\gamma+1
.

6.
1–mode initial data

In this section, we study the operators
\mathcal{M}
and
{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}
(Section
2
) for 1–mode initial data. Specifically, we

•

verify that all the 1–modes are zeros of the operators
\mathcal{M}
(for CW and CH) and
{\swabfamily M}_{-}
(for YM),

•

compute the differentials
d\mathcal{M}
and
d{\swabfamily M}_{-}
at the 1–mode initial data.

6.1.
Conformal cubic wave equation in spherical symmetry

Recall that the eigenfunctions
\{e_{n}:n\geq 0\}
are given by (
3.2
) and the PDE in the Fourier space from (
4.2
) reads

\displaystyle\ddot{u}^{m}(t)+\left(Au(t)\right)^{m}=\left(f\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m},\hskip 5.0ptm\geq 0

where the dots denote derivatives with respect to time and

\displaystyle\left(Au(t)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle:=\omega_{m}^{2}u^{m}(t),

\displaystyle\left(f(\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m}

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{i,j,k=0}^{\infty}C_{ijkm}u^{i}(t)u^{j}(t)u^{k}(t).

For any initial data

\displaystyle u(0,\cdot)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi^{n}e_{n},\hskip 5.0pt\xi=\{\xi^{n}:n\geq 0\},

we denote by

\displaystyle\Phi^{t}(\xi)=\left\{\xi^{n}\cos(\omega_{n}t):n\geq 0\right\}

the linear flow, that is the solution to the linear problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ddot{u}^{n}(t)+\omega_{n}^{2}u^{n}(t)=0,\hskip 5.0ptt\in\mathbb{R}\\
u^{n}(0)=\xi^{n},\hskip 5.0pt\dot{u}^{n}(0)=0.\end{cases}

For this model, we aim towards implementing the original version of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem (Theorem
2.4
) and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left(\xi\right):=A\xi+\langle f\rangle(\xi),\hskip 5.0pt\langle f\rangle(\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Phi^{t}\left[f\left(\Phi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]dt.

To begin with, we show that the 1–modes are zeros of the operator
\mathcal{M}
.

Lemma 6.1

(Zeros of the operator
\mathcal{M}
)
.

Let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
be the rescaled 1–mode initial data,

\displaystyle\xi^{m}:=K_{\gamma}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),\hskip 5.0ptm\geq 0,

(6.1)

where

\displaystyle K_{\gamma}=\pm 2\omega_{\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}}.

(6.2)

Then, we have
\mathcal{M}(\xi)=0
.

Proof.

Let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
be given by (
6.1
)–(
6.2
) and pick any integer
m\geq 0
. Then, we compute

\displaystyle(A\xi)^{m}

\displaystyle=\omega_{m}^{2}\xi^{m}=K_{\gamma}\omega_{\gamma}^{2}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),

\displaystyle(\Phi^{t}(\xi))^{m}

\displaystyle=\xi^{m}\cos(\omega_{m}t)=K_{\gamma}\cos(\omega_{\gamma}t)\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),

\displaystyle\left(f\left(\Phi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i,j,k}C_{ijkm}(\Phi^{t}(\xi))^{i}(\Phi^{t}(\xi))^{j}(\Phi^{t}(\xi))^{k}=-K_{\gamma}^{3}C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}\cos^{3}(\omega_{\gamma}t),

\displaystyle\left(\Phi^{t}\left[f\left(\Phi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=(f\left(\Phi^{t}(\xi)\right))^{m}\cos(\omega_{m}t)=-K_{\gamma}^{3}C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}\cos^{3}(\omega_{\gamma}t)\cos(\omega_{m}t),

\displaystyle\left(\langle f\rangle(\xi)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}}{2\pi}K_{\gamma}^{3}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos^{3}(\omega_{\gamma}t)\cos(\omega_{m}t)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}}{2\pi}K_{\gamma}^{3}\Bigg(\frac{3}{8}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\left((\omega_{m}+\omega_{\gamma})t\right)dt+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\left((\omega_{m}+3\omega_{\gamma})t\right)dt

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{8}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\left((\omega_{m}-\omega_{\gamma})t\right)dt+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\left((\omega_{m}-3\omega_{\gamma})tdt\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=-C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}K_{\gamma}^{3}\Bigg(\frac{3}{8}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma)+\frac{1}{8}\mathds{1}(m=3\gamma+2)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}{8}K_{\gamma}^{3}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),

\displaystyle(\mathcal{M}\left(\xi\right))^{m}

\displaystyle=(A\xi)^{m}+\left(\langle f\rangle(\xi)\right)^{m}=K_{\gamma}\left(\omega_{\gamma}^{2}-\frac{3C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}{8}K_{\gamma}^{2}\right)\mathds{1}(m=\gamma)=0,

where we used the fact that

\displaystyle\omega_{m}+\omega_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\neq 0,\hskip 5.0pt\omega_{m}+3\omega_{\gamma}\neq 0,

\displaystyle\omega_{m}-\omega_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=0\Longleftrightarrow m=\gamma,

\displaystyle\omega_{m}-3\omega_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=0\Longleftrightarrow m=3\gamma+2,

as well as
C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}=0
for
m=3\gamma+2
according to Lemma
5.1
.
∎

Next, we derive the differential of
\mathcal{M}
at the rescaled 1–modes.

Lemma 6.2

(Differential of
\mathcal{M}
at the 1–modes)
.

Let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
be given by (
6.1
)–(
6.2
). Then, for all
h=\{h^{j}:j\geq 0\}\in l_{s+3}^{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}(d\mathcal{M}(\xi)[h])^{m}

\displaystyle=\left[\left(\omega_{m}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}-2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma mm}\right)h^{m}-\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}h^{2\gamma-m}\right]\mathds{1}\left(0\leq m\leq\gamma-1\right)

\displaystyle+\left[-2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}h^{\gamma}\right]\mathds{1}\left(m=\gamma\right)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\omega_{m}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}-2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma mm}\right)h^{m}-\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}h^{2\gamma-m}\right]\mathds{1}\left(\gamma+1\leq m\leq 2\gamma\right)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\omega_{m}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}-2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma mm}\right)h^{m}\right]\mathds{1}\left(m\geq 2\gamma+1\right),

where
C_{ijkm}
are given in closed formulas in Lemma
5.2
.

Proof.

Let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
be given by (
6.1
)–(
6.2
),
\epsilon>0
,
h=\{h^{j}:j\geq 0\}\in l_{s+3}^{2}
and pick any integer
m\geq 0
. Then, using the symmetries of the Fourier coefficients, we compute

\displaystyle(A(\xi+\epsilon h))^{m}

\displaystyle=\omega_{m}^{2}(\xi^{m}+\epsilon h^{m})=(A\xi)^{m}+\epsilon\omega_{m}^{2}h^{m},

\displaystyle(\Phi^{t}(\xi+\epsilon h))^{m}

\displaystyle=(\xi^{m}+\epsilon h^{m})\cos(\omega_{m}t)=(\Phi^{t}(\xi))^{m}+\epsilon h^{m}\cos(\omega_{m}t),

\displaystyle\left(f\left(\Phi^{t}(\xi+\epsilon h)\right)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i,j,k}C_{ijkm}(\Phi^{t}(\xi+\epsilon h))^{i}(\Phi^{t}(\xi))^{j}(\Phi^{t}(\xi+\epsilon h))^{k}

\displaystyle=\left(f\left(\Phi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right)^{m}-3\epsilon K_{\gamma}^{2}\sum_{k}C_{\gamma\gamma km}h^{k}\cos^{2}(\omega_{\gamma}t)\cos(\omega_{k}t)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle\left(\Phi^{t}\left[f\left(\Phi^{t}(\xi+\epsilon h)\right)\right]\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=\left(\Phi^{t}\left[f\left(\Phi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]\right)^{m}-3\epsilon K_{\gamma}^{2}\sum_{i}C_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\cos^{2}(\omega_{\gamma}t)\cos(\omega_{i}t)\cos(\omega_{m}t)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle\left(\langle f\rangle(\xi+\epsilon h)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=\left(\langle f\rangle(\xi)\right)^{m}-\frac{3\epsilon K_{\gamma}^{2}}{2\pi}\sum_{i}C_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos^{2}(\omega_{\gamma}t)\cos(\omega_{i}t)\cos(\omega_{m}t)dt+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}).

Therefore, we infer

\displaystyle\left(d\langle f\rangle(\xi)[h]\right)^{m}=-\frac{3K_{\gamma}^{2}}{2\pi}\sum_{i}C_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos^{2}(\omega_{\gamma}t)\cos(\omega_{i}t)\cos(\omega_{m}t)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{3K_{\gamma}^{2}}{16\pi}\sum_{i}C_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\sum_{\pm}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos((\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{\gamma}\pm\omega_{\gamma}\pm\omega_{m})t)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{3K_{\gamma}^{2}}{8}\sum_{i}C_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\sum_{\pm}\mathds{1}(\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{\gamma}\pm\omega_{\gamma}\pm\omega_{m}=0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3K_{\gamma}^{2}}{8}\left[\sum_{i}C_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\mathds{1}(i=m)+\sum_{i}C_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\mathds{1}(i=m)+\sum_{i}C_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\mathds{1}(i=2\gamma-m\geq 0)\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{3K_{\gamma}^{2}}{8}\left[2C_{m\gamma\gamma m}h^{m}+C_{2\gamma-m,\gamma,\gamma,m}h^{2\gamma-m}\mathds{1}(0\leq m\leq 2\gamma)\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{\omega_{\gamma}^{2}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}\left[2C_{\gamma\gamma mm}h^{m}+\mathds{1}\left(0\leq m\leq 2\gamma\right)C_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}h^{2\gamma-m}\right],

where we used the fact that
C_{ijkm}=0
for
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{k}\pm\omega_{m}=0
with only 1 minus sign according to Lemma
5.1
, that is

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\omega_{i}+\omega_{\gamma}+\omega_{\gamma}+\omega_{m}=0,\\
\omega_{i}-\omega_{\gamma}+\omega_{\gamma}+\omega_{m}=0,\\
\omega_{i}+\omega_{\gamma}-\omega_{\gamma}+\omega_{m}=0,\\
\omega_{i}+\omega_{\gamma}+\omega_{\gamma}-\omega_{m}=0,\end{cases}

so we are left with
\omega_{i}\pm\omega_{j}\pm\omega_{k}\pm\omega_{m}=0
with only 2 minus signs and there are three such terms in total, that is

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\omega_{i}+\omega_{\gamma}-\omega_{\gamma}-\omega_{m}=0,\\
\omega_{i}-\omega_{\gamma}+\omega_{\gamma}-\omega_{m}=0,\\
\omega_{i}-\omega_{\gamma}-\omega_{\gamma}+\omega_{m}=0,\\
\end{cases}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}i=m,\\
i=m,\\
i=2\gamma-m\text{ and }i\geq 0.\\
\end{cases}

Finally, we obtain

\displaystyle(d\mathcal{M}(\xi)[h])^{m}

\displaystyle=\omega_{m}^{2}h^{m}+\left(d\langle f\rangle(\xi)[h]\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=\left[\omega_{m}^{2}-\frac{2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma mm}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}\right]h^{m}-\mathds{1}\left(0\leq m\leq 2\gamma\right)\frac{\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}h^{2\gamma-m}

\displaystyle=\left[\omega_{m}^{2}-\frac{2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma mm}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}\right]h^{m}-\mathds{1}\left(0\leq m\leq 2\gamma\right)\frac{\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}h^{2\gamma-m}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}\left(0\leq m\leq\gamma-1\right)\left[\left(\omega_{m}^{2}-\frac{2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma mm}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}\right)h^{m}-\frac{\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}h^{2\gamma-m}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathds{1}\left(m=\gamma\right)\left[-2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}h^{\gamma}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathds{1}\left(\gamma+1\leq m\leq 2\gamma\right)\left[\left(\omega_{m}^{2}-\frac{2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma mm}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}\right)h^{m}-\frac{\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}h^{2\gamma-m}\right]

\displaystyle+\mathds{1}\left(m\geq 2\gamma+1\right)\left[\left(\omega_{m}^{2}-\frac{2\omega_{\gamma}^{2}C_{\gamma\gamma mm}}{C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}\right)h^{m}\right],

that completes the proof.
∎

6.2.
Conformal cubic wave equation out of spherical symmetry

Recall that the eigenfunctions
\{\mathsf{e}_{n}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}:n\geq 0\}
are given by (
3.4
) and the PDE in the Fourier space from (
4.5
) reads

\displaystyle\ddot{u}^{m}(t)+\left(\mathsf{A}u(t)\right)^{m}=\left(\mathsf{f}(\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m},\hskip 5.0ptm\geq 0

where the dots denote derivatives with respect to time and

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{A}u(t)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle:=\left(\somega_{n}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\right)^{2}u^{m}(t),

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{f}(\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m}

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{i,j,k=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{C}_{ijkm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}u^{i}(t)u^{j}(t)u^{k}(t).

For any initial data

\displaystyle u(0,\cdot)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi^{n}\mathsf{e}_{n}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})},\hskip 5.0pt\xi=\{\xi^{n}:n\geq 0\},

we denote by

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Phi}^{t}(\xi)=\left\{\xi^{n}\cos(\somega_{n}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}t):n\geq 0\right\}

the linear flow, that is the solution to the linear problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ddot{u}^{n}(t)+\left(\somega_{n}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\right)^{2}u^{n}(t)=0,\hskip 5.0ptt\in\mathbb{R}\\
u^{n}(0)=\xi^{n},\hskip 5.0pt\dot{u}(0)=0.\end{cases}

For this model, we aim towards implementing the original version of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem (Theorem
2.4
) and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\left(\xi\right):=\mathsf{A}\xi+\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle(\xi),\hskip 5.0pt\langle\mathsf{f}\rangle(\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathsf{\Phi}^{t}\left[f\left(\mathsf{\Phi}^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]dt.

To begin with, we show that the 1–modes are zeros of the operator
\mathcal{M}
.

Lemma 6.3

(Zeros of the operator
\mathcal{M}
)
.

Let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
be the rescaled 1–mode initial data,

\displaystyle\xi^{m}:=\mathsf{K}_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),\hskip 5.0ptm\geq 0,

(6.3)

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{K}_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=\pm 2\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\sqrt{\frac{2}{3C_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}}}.

(6.4)

Then, we have
\mathcal{M}(\xi)=0
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Lemma
6.1
.
∎

Next, we derive the differential of
\mathcal{M}
at the rescaled 1–modes.

Lemma 6.4

(Differential of
\mathcal{M}
at the 1–modes)
.

Let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
be given by (
6.3
)–(
6.4
). Then, for all
h=\{h^{j}:j\geq 0\}\in l_{s+3}^{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}(d\mathcal{M}(\xi)[h])^{m}

\displaystyle=\left[\left(\somega_{m}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-2\somega_{\gamma}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\right)h^{m}-\somega_{\gamma}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}h^{2\gamma-m}\right]\mathds{1}\left(0\leq m\leq\gamma-1\right)

\displaystyle+\left[-2\somega_{\gamma}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}h^{\gamma}\right]\mathds{1}\left(m=\gamma\right)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\somega_{m}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-2\somega_{\gamma}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\right)h^{m}-\somega_{\gamma}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}h^{2\gamma-m}\right]\mathds{1}\left(\gamma+1\leq m\leq 2\gamma\right)

\displaystyle+\left[\left(\somega_{m}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-2\somega_{\gamma}^{2}\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\right)h^{m}\right]\mathds{1}\left(m\geq 2\gamma+1\right),

where
\mathsf{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}
are given by closed formulas in Lemma
5.4
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Lemma
6.2
due to the fact that
\mathsf{C}_{ijkm}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0
for
\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0
with only 1 minus sign according to Lemma
5.3
, that is

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,\\
\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,\\
\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,\\
\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,\end{cases}

so we are left with
\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{j}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{k}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}\pm\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0
with only 2 minus signs and there are again the same three such terms in total, that is

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,\\
\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,\\
\somega_{i}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}-\somega_{\gamma}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}+\somega_{m}^{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})}=0,\\
\end{cases}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}i=m,\\
i=m,\\
i=2\gamma-m\text{ and }i\geq 0,\\
\end{cases}

that completes the proof.
∎

6.3.
Yang–Mills equation in spherical symmetry

Recall that the eigenfunctions
\{\mathfrak{e}_{n}:n\geq 0\}
are given by (
3.7
) and the PDE in the Fourier space from (
4.8
) reads

\displaystyle\ddot{u}^{m}(t)+\left(\mathfrak{A}u(t)\right)^{m}=\left(\mathfrak{f}(\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m},\hskip 5.0ptm\geq 0

where the dots denote derivatives with respect to time and

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{A}u(t)\right)^{m}:=\ssomega_{n}^{2}u^{m}(t),\hskip 5.0pt\left(\mathfrak{f}(u)\right)^{m}:=\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(u)\right)^{m}+\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(u)\right)^{m},

with

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}(\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m}

\displaystyle:=-3\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}u^{i}(t)u^{j}(t),

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}(\{u^{j}(t):j\geq 0\})\right)^{m}

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{i,j,k=0}^{\infty}\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}u^{i}(t)u^{j}(t)u^{k}(t).

For any initial data

\displaystyle u(0,\cdot)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\xi^{n}\mathfrak{e}_{n},\hskip 5.0pt\xi=\{\xi^{n}:n\geq 0\},

we denote by

\displaystyle\pPhi^{t}(\xi)=\left\{\xi^{n}\cos(\ssomega_{n}t):n\geq 0\right\}

the linear flow, that is the solution to the linear problem

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ddot{u}^{n}(t)+\ssomega_{n}^{2}u^{n}(t)=0,\hskip 5.0ptt\in\mathbb{R}\\
u^{n}(0)=\xi^{n},\hskip 5.0pt\dot{u}^{n}(0)=0.\end{cases}

As a starting point, we show that the original version of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem (Theorem
2.4
) is not applicable.

Lemma 6.5

(Non–resonant
\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}
)
.

For all initial data
\xi\in l_{s}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\rangle(\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\pPhi^{t}\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]dt=0.

Proof.

Let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}\in l_{s}^{2}
be any initial data and pick an integer
m\geq 0
. We compute

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{A}\xi)^{m}

\displaystyle=\ssomega_{m}^{2}\xi^{m},

\displaystyle(\pPhi^{t}(\xi))^{m}

\displaystyle=\xi^{m}\cos(\ssomega_{m}t),

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-3\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}(\pPhi^{t}(\xi))^{i}(\pPhi^{t}(\xi))^{j}=-3\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\cos(\ssomega_{i}t)\cos(\ssomega_{j}t)

\displaystyle\left(\pPhi^{t}\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{f}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right))^{m}\cos(\ssomega_{m}t)=-3\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\prod_{\lambda\in\{i,j,m\}}\cos(\ssomega_{\lambda}t),

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(2)}\rangle(\xi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2\pi}\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\prod_{\lambda\in\{i,j,m\}}\cos(\ssomega_{\lambda}t)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2\pi}\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pm\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{4}\cos\left(\ssomega_{i}\pm\ssomega_{j}\pm\ssomega_{m}\right)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{i,j=0}^{\infty}\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}\xi^{i}\xi^{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pm\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}\left(\ssomega_{i}\pm\ssomega_{j}\pm\ssomega_{m}=0\right).

Now, notice that all the possible conditions are those with only 1 minus sign, namely

\displaystyle-\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{m}=0,

\displaystyle+\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{j}+\ssomega_{m}=0,

\displaystyle+\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{j}-\ssomega_{m}=0,

and according to Lemma
5.7
the corresponding Fourier coefficients vanish.
∎

Consequently, for this model, we aim towards implementing the modified version of Bambusi–Paleari’s theorem (Theorem
2.5
) and define

\displaystyle{\swabfamily M}_{\pm}(\xi)

\displaystyle=\pm\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)+\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi),\hskip 5.0pt\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\pPhi^{t}\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]dt,

where
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)
is given for any initial data by Lemma
2.13
and for the 1–mode initial data by Lemma
2.14
. Also, recall the Diophantine condition
\ssomega\in\mathcal{W}_{\alpha}
for some
0<\alpha<1/3
from Theorem
2.5
.

To begin with, we show that the 1–modes are zeros of the operator
{\swabfamily M}_{-}
.

Lemma 6.6

(Zeros of the operator
{\swabfamily M}_{-}
)
.

Let
\gamma\in\{0,1,\dots,5\}
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma}:=\frac{9}{4}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\gamma\gamma\nu}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{2}{\ssomega_{\nu}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\ssomega_{\nu}^{2}-(2\ssomega_{\gamma})^{2}}\right).

Then, we have that
8\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma}>3\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}
. Moreover, let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
to be the rescaled 1–mode initial data,

\displaystyle\xi^{m}:=\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),\hskip 5.0ptm\geq 0,

(6.5)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}:=\pm 2\somega_{\gamma}\sqrt{\frac{2}{8\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma}-3\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}}.

(6.6)

Then, we have that
{\swabfamily M}_{-}(\xi)=0
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in\{0,1,\dots,5\}
, define
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
to be the rescaled 1–mode initial data given by (
6.5
)–(
6.6
) and pick any integer
m\geq 0
. Firstly, we compute
-\mathfrak{A}\xi+\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)
. We have

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{A}\xi)^{m}

\displaystyle=\ssomega_{m}^{2}\xi^{m}=\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}\ssomega_{\gamma}^{2}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),

\displaystyle(\pPhi^{t}(\xi))^{m}

\displaystyle=\xi^{m}\cos(\ssomega_{m}t)=\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}\cos(\ssomega_{\gamma}t)\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i,j,k}\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}(\pPhi^{t}(\xi))^{i}(\pPhi^{t}(\xi))^{j}(\pPhi^{t}(\xi))^{k}=-\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{3}\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}\cos^{3}(\ssomega_{\gamma}t),

\displaystyle\left(\pPhi^{t}\left[\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right)\right]\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\left(\pPhi^{t}(\xi)\right))^{m}\cos(\ssomega_{m}t)=-\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{3}\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}\cos^{3}(\ssomega_{\gamma}t)\cos(\ssomega_{m}t),

\displaystyle\left(\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}}{2\pi}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{3}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos^{3}(\ssomega_{\gamma}t)\cos(\ssomega_{m}t)dt

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}}{2\pi}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{3}\Bigg(\frac{3}{8}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\left((\ssomega_{m}+\ssomega_{\gamma})t\right)dt+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\left((\ssomega_{m}+3\ssomega_{\gamma})t\right)dt

\displaystyle+\frac{3}{8}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\left((\ssomega_{m}-\ssomega_{\gamma})t\right)dt+\frac{1}{8}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\cos\left((\ssomega_{m}-3\ssomega_{\gamma})t\right)dt\Bigg)

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{3}\Bigg(\frac{3}{8}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma)+\frac{1}{8}\mathds{1}(m=3\gamma+4)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}{8}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{3}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),

\displaystyle-(\mathfrak{A}\xi)^{m}+\left(\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}\left(\ssomega_{\gamma}^{2}+\frac{3\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}{8}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}\right)\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),

where we used the fact that

\displaystyle\ssomega_{m}+\ssomega_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\neq 0,\hskip 5.0pt\ssomega_{m}+3\ssomega_{\gamma}\neq 0,

\displaystyle\ssomega_{m}-\ssomega_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=0\Longleftrightarrow m=\gamma,

\displaystyle\ssomega_{m}-3\ssomega_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=0\Longleftrightarrow m=3\gamma+4,

as well as
\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma m}=0
for
m=3\gamma+4
according to Lemma
5.7
. Furthermore, we use the computation for
\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)
at the 1–mode initial data we derived in Lemma
2.14
, that is

\displaystyle\left(\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)\right)^{m}=\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{3}\mathds{1}(m=\gamma),

with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma}:=\frac{9}{4}\sum_{\nu=0}^{2\gamma}\left(\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{\gamma\gamma\nu}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{2}{\ssomega_{\nu}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\ssomega_{\nu}^{2}-(2\ssomega_{\gamma})^{2}}\right),

to conclude that

\displaystyle({\swabfamily M}_{-}\left(\xi\right))^{m}

\displaystyle=-(\mathfrak{A}\xi)^{m}+\left(\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)\right)^{m}+\left(\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}\left(\ssomega_{\gamma}^{2}+\frac{3\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}}{8}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}-\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}\right)\mathds{1}(m=\gamma)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{8}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}\left(8\ssomega_{\gamma}^{2}+3\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}-8\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}\right)\mathds{1}(m=\gamma)

\displaystyle=0,

provided that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}=\frac{-8\ssomega_{\gamma}^{2}}{3\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma\gamma\gamma}-8\mathfrak{q}_{\gamma}}.

Finally, it remains to show that this choice is well defined, that is
\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}\in\mathbb{R}
, for all
\gamma\in\{0,1,\dots,10\}
. To this end, we fix
\gamma\in\{0,1,\dots,10\}
and use the definition of the Fourier coefficients to compute each
\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}
and verify that they are all real numbers. Figure
3
illustrates the constants
\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}
as
\gamma
varies within
\{0,1,\dots,10\}
.

Figure 3.
The constants
\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}
as
\gamma
varies within
\{0,1,\dots,10\}
. They are all real numbers.

∎

Next, we derive the differential of
{\swabfamily M}_{-}
at the rescaled 1–modes.

Lemma 6.7

(Differential of
{\swabfamily M}_{-}
at the 1–modes)
.

Let
\gamma\in\{0,1,\dots,10\}
and let
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
be given by (
6.5
)–(
6.6
). Then, for all
h=\{h^{j}:j\geq 0\}\in l_{s+3}^{2}
, we have that

\displaystyle-\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{-2}(d{\swabfamily M}_{-}(\xi)[h])^{m}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}(0\leq m\leq\gamma-1)\Bigg[h^{m}\mathfrak{u}_{\gamma m}+h^{2\gamma-m}\mathfrak{v}_{\gamma m}\Bigg]+\mathds{1}(m=\gamma)\Bigg[h^{\gamma}\left(\mathfrak{u}_{\gamma\gamma}+\mathfrak{v}_{\gamma\gamma})\right)\Bigg]

\displaystyle+\mathds{1}(\gamma+1\leq m\leq 2\gamma)\Bigg[h^{m}\mathfrak{u}_{\gamma m}+h^{2\gamma-m}\mathfrak{v}_{\gamma m}\Bigg]+\mathds{1}(m\geq 2\gamma+1)\Bigg[h^{m}\mathfrak{u}_{\gamma m}\Bigg],

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}_{\gamma m}:=\left(\frac{\ssomega_{m}}{\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}}\right)^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}-\mathfrak{a}_{\gamma m},\hskip 5.0pt\mathfrak{v}_{\gamma m}:=\frac{3}{8}\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}-\mathfrak{b}_{\gamma m},

and
\overline{\mathfrak{C}}_{ijm}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}
are given respectively by Lemmata
5.8
and
5.10
whereas
\mathfrak{a}_{\gamma m}
and
\mathfrak{b}_{\gamma m}
are given by Lemma
2.15
.

Proof.

Let
\gamma\in\{0,1,\dots,10\}
,
\xi=\{\xi^{m}:m\geq 0\}
be given by (
6.5
)–(
6.6
),
h=\{h^{j}:j\geq 0\}\in l_{s+3}^{2}
and pick any integer
m\geq 0
. Firstly, we use similar computations to the ones derived in Lemma
6.2
, to obtain

\displaystyle\left(d\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)[h]\right)^{m}=-\frac{3\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}}{8}\sum_{i}\mathfrak{C}_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\sum_{\pm}\mathds{1}(\ssomega_{i}\pm\ssomega_{\gamma}\pm\ssomega_{\gamma}\pm\ssomega_{m}=0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}}{8}\left[\sum_{i}\mathfrak{C}_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\mathds{1}(i=m)+\sum_{i}\mathfrak{C}_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\mathds{1}(i=m)+\sum_{i}\mathfrak{C}_{i\gamma\gamma m}h^{i}\mathds{1}(i=2\gamma-m\geq 0)\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{3\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}}{8}\left[2\mathfrak{C}_{m\gamma\gamma m}h^{m}+\mathfrak{C}_{2\gamma-m,\gamma,\gamma,m}h^{2\gamma-m}\mathds{1}(0\leq m\leq 2\gamma)\right],

where we used the fact that
\mathfrak{C}_{ijkm}=0
for
\ssomega_{i}\pm\ssomega_{j}\pm\ssomega_{k}\pm\ssomega_{m}=0
with only 1 minus sign according to Lemma
5.7
, that is

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{\gamma}+\ssomega_{\gamma}+\ssomega_{m}=0,\\
\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{\gamma}+\ssomega_{\gamma}+\ssomega_{m}=0,\\
\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{\gamma}-\ssomega_{\gamma}+\ssomega_{m}=0,\\
\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{\gamma}+\ssomega_{\gamma}-\ssomega_{m}=0,\end{cases}

so we are left with
\ssomega_{i}\pm\ssomega_{j}\pm\ssomega_{k}\pm\ssomega_{m}=0
with only 2 minus signs and there are three such terms in total, that is

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\ssomega_{i}+\ssomega_{\gamma}-\ssomega_{\gamma}-\ssomega_{m}=0,\\
\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{\gamma}+\ssomega_{\gamma}-\ssomega_{m}=0,\\
\ssomega_{i}-\ssomega_{\gamma}-\ssomega_{\gamma}+\ssomega_{m}=0,\\
\end{cases}\Longleftrightarrow\begin{cases}i=m,\\
i=m,\\
i=2\gamma-m\text{ and }i\geq 0.\\
\end{cases}

Then, we infer

\displaystyle-\left(d\mathfrak{A}\xi[h]\right)^{m}+\left(d\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)[h]\right)^{m}=-\ssomega_{m}^{2}h^{m}+\left(d\langle\mathfrak{f}^{(3)}\rangle(\xi)[h]\right)^{m}

\displaystyle=-\ssomega_{m}^{2}h^{m}-\frac{3\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}}{8}\left[2\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}h^{m}+\mathds{1}\left(0\leq m\leq 2\gamma\right)\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}h^{2\gamma-m}\right]

\displaystyle=-\left[\ssomega_{m}^{2}+\frac{3}{4}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma\gamma mm}\right]h^{m}-\mathds{1}\left(0\leq m\leq 2\gamma\right)\frac{3}{8}\mathfrak{K}_{\gamma}^{2}\mathfrak{C}_{\gamma,2\gamma-m,\gamma,m}h^{2\gamma-m}.

Recall that the differential of
\mathfrak{F}_{0}
at the 1–modes is given by Lemma
2.15
, that is

\displaystyle\left(d\mathfrak{F}_{0}(\xi)[h]\right)^{m}
